        Severity: high, sustained economic coercion across multiple victims. Pattern: undeniable, seven directors, eighteen months, four districts. Reversibility: limited; competitors driven from markets do not recover their position. Three axes. Qualifying event. Reassignment to -1.

        She called her general counsel, Ruth Anselm, up from the floor below and opened her own implant logs on the desk screen between them. They held performance reviews, strategy meetings and compensation decisions, and nothing else.

        "I never gave an order," Maren said.

        Ruth read both pages. "It doesn't mention orders."

        "Then what does it look at?"

        "Who benefited. How consistently. Whether the correlation exceeds chance." Ruth turned back to the first page. "It does."

        Teodor and the other six directors stayed in Main Layer, each with their own STI violations and a damaged trust score. Maren went to -1, permanently, and the bonuses, the promotions and the social access left Main Layer with her. The network dissolved.

        Key lesson: A leader who consistently profits from harm her associates commit carries the pattern on her own ledger. No conspiracy trial or judge is needed to find it.

        Dav Orent had been reassigned from Main Layer to -1 Noncompliance nine years earlier, for a pattern of financial fraud, and in -1 he rebuilt: private contracts, a crew of forty, three district-level building projects. He was a district construction magnate now, and wealthy by -1 standards.

        At a contractor meeting, in front of thirty witnesses, a disgruntled worker named Asha Mirin leaned across the table and spat in his face. The ledger logged it as a minor assault: low severity, isolated, fully reversible. One axis. Asha's STI took a hit.

        "It's corrective," Pell, his site manager, said that evening. "A fine, some stigma. Maybe she loses her current contract."

        "She spat in my face in front of thirty people," Dav said.

        In the morning he filed charges with Oren Tace, one of the district's private court operators. They made up a network of arbitrators who resolved commercial disputes for fees, and affiliated private security firms enforced their rulings. Tace had adjudicated six of Dav's commercial disputes in the past two years and depended on his continued business. He sentenced Asha to three hundred years in a private detention facility run by Dav's security affiliate.

        "It meets minimum -1 standards," Tace said. "Basic rations, restricted movement, no physical harm."

        Then the affiliate's duty officer called.

        "It's flagged," she said. "Unenforceable."

        "Flagged by what?"

        "The AI's proportionality check. It runs automatically, on every consequence in -1. Private ones too."

        "On a private court's sentence?"

        "Three hundred years against a one-axis offense," she said. "The axis profile doesn't match."

        Asha's consequence went back to the proportional one: the STI hit, the fine, and whatever the market decided about her contracts.

        Dav went to a different arbitrator, who framed the charge as breach of professional conduct and gave her fifty years. That sentence came back flagged and unenforceable, like the first. The third time he filed through a commercial mediation channel and asked for her indefinite exclusion from all district construction contracts. He controlled most of the work in the district.

        This time the AI read the three filings together: three attempts to impose disproportionate consequence on one person for one minor offense. The pattern axis activated on Dav's own ledger.

        Six months later the notice came. His accumulated pattern of coercive private court manipulation had crossed the two-axis threshold. It went beyond the Asha case: the network attribution system now correlated a history of similar filings against contractors, suppliers and former employees.

        Severity: sustained economic coercion of multiple individuals. Pattern: documented across years.

        He faced formal multi-factor evaluation for -2 reassignment.

        Key lesson: In -1 every consequence, private ones included, has to fit the offense. Pushing for more builds a pattern on the filer's own ledger.

        Dren Vasic lived in -2's eastern industrial corridor, inside the eleven blocks the Reth Syndicate controlled. The Syndicate ran resource distribution, private lending and territorial security for about 9,000 residents there, with an enforcement arm of forty operators. Dren paid its territorial fees and had never borrowed from it.

        Her neighbor Kolev Rast had. He owed the Syndicate $340,000 in accumulated lending principal and territorial fees, and he could not pay. He had no crew affiliation, no district standing and no leverage. One night Dren watched from her window as two Syndicate operators took him from his unit. He was still talking as they walked him off. "End of the month," he kept saying. "End of the month."

        Every resident of the eastern corridor knew what happened in block seven. Three years earlier the Syndicate had acquired portable backup vessel fabrication capacity in a private deal with a -2 tech broker. The units were crude, with revival fidelity of approximately 97.5% per event, lower than VMSS fabrication proxy installations, but they were mobile and private. The Syndicate did not advertise what it used them for.

        Eleven days later Kolev came back down the lane pushing a Syndicate distribution cart. His hands were whole. That evening Dren took him soup.

        "The basement in block seven," he said, before she could ask. "They broke both my hands the first night. When the pain got unbearable, I died. On purpose."

        A bailout. Dren knew the backup vessel system should have revived him at the nearest VMSS fabrication proxy installation and reset his physical circumstances.

        "Their portable unit intercepted the revival. I woke up in the same basement," Kolev said. "Same operators. Hands intact." He flexed them. "The debt was still there, so they started again. I died a second time, and a third. Same room every time."

        "How many times?" Dren asked.

        "Nine. Their unit fails one revival in forty. That was the only way out I had, and at three revivals a week, the odds said it would take months of sustained torture. So I stopped resisting and took their terms."

        "What terms?"

        "Eighteen months unpaid on their distribution network. The debt's forgiven when I finish, and I'm bound to the eleven blocks for five years." He nodded at the cart. "It's better than the basement."

        In the corridor debts were paid on time, and territorial fees went uncontested. The forty operators hardly ever came down Dren's lane.

        Dren had no debts and a clean -2 record. She also had no crew, no district standing and no guarantee that the Syndicate's lending terms wouldn't find her eventually. She began researching voluntary permanent residency in -3.

        What drew her was one thing. In -3 death was final, and no one would own her continuity. The Syndicate couldn't follow her there; its operators would lose revival access the moment they crossed into Terminal. She went over it every night: lower UBI, no institutional floor, no revival if she was killed. No basement, and no one reviving her into the same room.

        She hasn't decided yet. Most mornings she watches Kolev take the cart out.

        Key lesson: -2 sets no institutional limit on nonlethal harm, and a private revival unit can make that harm indefinite. Voluntary permanent residency in -3, where death is final, exists for that reason.

        For six years Yael Breck lived in -2's Korrath Metropolitan Zone, a fortified district of 140,000 residents run by three competing private security cooperatives. The metro worked: paved roads, a private power grid, a functioning market, a reputation-gated residential core. Life there resembled a rougher -1. Yael, an equipment technician for the largest cooperative, was uncomfortable and stable, with crew affiliation, district standing and a two-room unit in the core.

        The trouble began as a contract dispute with his cooperative's logistics chief, Varga, a man with seniority and connections.

        "That's not what the contract says," Yael said.

        "It says what I say it says."

        It escalated into a fistfight, and both of them took STI hits. Within two weeks Yael's contract was terminated. The district association revoked his residential access on the chief's recommendation, which carried weight.

        He moved to the metro's outer ring. Three months later came a second dispute, with a market vendor who accused him of equipment theft.

        "That's my compressor," the vendor said, loud enough for the whole row to hear.

        The accusation was logged on Yael's public ledger, and the dispute cost him his remaining reputation score in the Korrath network. After that no private operator in the metro would hire him.

        He drifted to the outskirts. The change happened over four blocks. Maintained roads gave way to cracked asphalt, and the cooperative's security patrols ended at the district boundary marker. Past it lay contested territory: bandit crews, and no reputation network or private infrastructure. Every territorial operator out there had access to the same tools the Syndicate cartels used in the corridors, portable backup vessel units included.

        He had seen what happened to unaffiliated residents in contested zones. He had watched extractions from a distance, once a man hauled out of a shed at dusk with the portable unit already on the truck, and he knew what the units were for. People without metro affiliation ended up in the outskirts, and the cartels sourced their unpaid labor there. If he stayed, he faced bandit crews with forced revival capability and the risk of indefinite capture, with no crew, no standing and no leverage.

        Or he could register voluntary permanent residency in -3. Its appeal was finality: no one would own his continuity. Death there would be final, with no revival loop, and no cartel could force him back into the same room. His dashboard showed UBI of $2,500 a month. In -3 it would drop to $1,250, with no backup vessel and no institutional floor.

        "Your ledger will say voluntary exit," said Osric, an old salvager who slept two doorways down. "Whoever's on top down there reads ledgers. The ones who fought or killed their way in will see a man who chose the easy door."

        "And the first days?"

        "Might be the worst of your life, and you don't come back from dying in them." Osric poked at the fire. "Or you kill somebody up here. They reassign you to -3 on a murderer's ledger instead of a coward's. Down there they respect that, or at least they don't target it."

        "And the one I kill?"

        "Gets revived in -2."

        Yael would still have killed someone, and crossed a line that would reshape his own psychology permanently.

        "It'd have to be real," Yael said. "The implant captures intent. Stage it with someone willing, and it's still murder on the ledger."

        On a Tuesday evening, after nine days of weighing all three, he stood at the boundary marker with the metro's lights behind him, looking into the dark of the outskirts.

        He chose -3. He walked to the nearest federal processing node and registered voluntary permanent residency. After the psychological screening, the transfer took forty minutes. He arrived in -3 with $600 in siloed currency, a technician's skill set and a ledger that read voluntary exit from -2. He did not know what the first night would look like.

        Key lesson: In -2, losing district standing means losing the only places the layer is survivable long-term. The options left after that are shaped by other residents, within conditions the system set.

        The network was informal: forty-three of the wealthiest citizens in Main Layer's District 7, coordinating on a call that met on Thursday evenings and kept no minutes. On the first evening Aurel Brandt shared a single page of figures.

        District 7 had 1.1 million residents. Its aggregate savings stood at $97 billion and were climbing. At $100 billion the Savings Circulation Mandate would trigger, and garnishing would start at 10% a month. Between them, the forty-three held $14.2 billion, 14.6% of the district's total.

        "We move enough of it out before the trigger activates," Aurel said. "Into things that can't be garnished. The whole district gets a delay, and we unwind it later."

        Petra Casimir read the page twice and said yes.

        For two weeks she spent like someone furnishing a new life. She prepaid service contracts far ahead, paid for property maintenance long before it was due, bought commodities in bulk, and commissioned an installation for her atrium from a sculptor named Ines Aro, who asked twice whether she was sure about the budget. Across the forty-three, $8 billion left savings accounts.

        The next Thursday Aurel posted the new page. The district aggregate read $89 billion. Someone laughed, and someone asked how soon they could start unwinding. Petra was looking at the line below it, the one the trigger actually read: the 90-day rolling average, $96.8 billion. Two weeks of spending had barely dented it.

        "That's today's number," she said. "The trigger doesn't read it."

        "If it did, we'd be done," Aurel said after a moment. "We'd unwind month by month, and nobody would ever see it come back into savings."

        "It reads ninety days. To hold the average under a hundred for the whole window, we keep fourteen billion out of savings for ninety consecutive days."

        They decided to keep the eight billion out and hope it was enough.

        For ninety days the eight billion stayed out, and Petra watched where it went: to contractors, artists, suppliers and service providers. Ines Aro's crew worked in her atrium all season. The contractors she had prepaid took on more staff. The rest of the district went on saving. Every Thursday Petra opened the rolling average, and every Thursday it had barely moved.

        On day ninety-one it crossed $100 billion anyway.

        Ines came that week to sign the finished piece. Standing under it, she told Petra it was the best-paid job of her working life and that she had put most of it into savings. Petra's maintenance contractor had done the same.

        The cycle activated, and the coalition's remaining savings, Petra's among them, were garnished at 10%. That evening she ran the figures for all forty-three. The ninety days of spending had cost them more than the garnishing would have.

        The next quarter, three members left the call, and nobody argued with them. By month six nobody was scheduling Thursdays, and the network had dissolved. The installation is still in Petra's atrium. She has grown fond of it.

        Key lesson: Holding a 90-day rolling average down means spending the money for ninety days, which is what the mandate wants. The system never has to detect the cartel.

        Judit Sarvas was a constitutional analyst, and this time her job was to be hostile. She was given the complete VMSS doctrine: the charter, the whitepaper, the governance architecture, the layer mechanics, the STI framework, the economic model, the enforcement infrastructure and the full simulation library. Her brief asked for structural failures and said what counted as one: a place where the architecture contradicted itself, an exploit the system could not prevent, or two doctrinal commitments colliding in a way that forced the system to violate one of them. Philosophical objections did not count. Neither did this seems harsh.

        She wrote her attacks into a review file, and a reply came back under each one.

        She opened with five attacks at the level of principle. Her STI flatline exploit and her constitutional deadlock were both dismissed: Article XII's multi-factor evaluation already prevented single-metric determination, and three different mechanisms handled three different categories of change. The abortion collision was dismissed as a philosophical objection dressed as structural failure. The reply accepted her other two, downward visitation asymmetry and unequal contestation capacity across layers, as real design tensions, and showed her where each was documented as a feature. The last line under the round read The architecture holds.

        In the second round she went after sequence failures: specific events where the system followed its own rules and the output contradicted a different rule. She started with wrongful reassignment. In her scenario the system misread a resident and reassigned them, and the permanence rule blocked the correction. She constructed a void-transaction reversal mechanism to repair it.

        The reply came back at once. AI governance operated as physics, it said, and no resident could repudiate the implant ledger. Wrongful conviction was not a category of appeal. Where the system's own audit verified institutional error, corrupted ledger data for one, the Supreme Court held remedial authority over that astronomically narrow case. Her mechanism contradicted the foundational axiom.

        Ansel Brandis, who shared her office, read it over her shoulder. "That's all they said?"

        "That's all." She was already chaining a late-recognized structural petition to the same reversal mechanism.

        It came back with the same contradiction. Her third attack argued that implant refusal at entry forced a contradiction between universality and voluntary refusal, and the reply said it contradicted five pages of established voluntary-implant doctrine. Her last two, it noted, miscategorized infrastructure prerequisites as behavioral metrics.

        For the third round she hunted success-state failures, scenarios where every rule worked correctly and the intended output still undermined the civilization's stated goals. Her first was risk-minimizing disengagement: the 10:1 penalty asymmetry made caution the rational strategy and suppressed ambitious contribution. The reply said the system optimized against harmful variance. Contribution was one of seven STI dimensions, and a cautious hermit flatlined and never reached Sanctuary.

        "So the hermit never gets there," Ansel said.

        She read him the next line of the reply. "The system is working as designed."

        Her campaign-by-visibility attack said public endorsement signals created a soft campaign advantage in a no-election system. The reply cited Section 5.6, which amplified trajectory without creating it; visibility accelerated an AI-observed direction and could not manufacture one. Her talent-sink attack said that skilled descenders under voluntary permanent residency made the lower layers attractive and diluted the punitive gradient. That came back as an explicitly documented feature, since the doctrine openly stated that the lower layers were opportunity as well as consequence.

        Her selection spiral had children fleeing the lower layers through child relocation and draining their stabilizing demographics. The reply answered that the child flight rate was itself the diagnostic that triggered systemic review. Last, she argued that revival gradients made -2 semi-terminal, since accumulated revival failures at 1:1K would produce permanent death over time. The reply pointed her to the technologies page, where the declining reliability gradient was published as intended design. The round closed like the two before it, on The architecture holds.

        In the fourth round she turned to self-monitoring verification: whether the feedback loops the doctrine described actually closed in time to prevent the damage they claimed to detect. She tested five mechanisms: the child flight diagnostic, STI flatline detection, the Meritboard structural petition filter, public signal correction and civil court contestation. All five were post-hoc. Each detected, annotated and redirected after first-wave damage had propagated. This time she was right.

        The reply said so, and called it consistent with the doctrine's foundational position: prevention existed only in Sanctuary, through pre-intervention, and everywhere else the system was consequence-based by design. Its feedback loops were forensic, corrective and adaptive, and none of them was preventive.

        One check was left. She went through the site's language page by page, auditing it for representational honesty and looking for any mechanism described as preventive where it was actually corrective.

        "Anything?" Ansel asked on the third day.

        "Not one."

        Her report opened with the count: four rounds, fifteen attacks, zero confirmed structural failures. Below it she listed what remained, the acknowledged design tensions, the explicitly intended harsh outcomes, and the self-monitoring stress points whose post-hoc nature was consistent with a consequence-based civilization.

        She kept the void-transaction reversal mechanism in a folder of its own.

        Key lesson: A design survives hostile review when it admits its brutal outcomes and names them as intended. VMSS does not claim to prevent all harm.

        Carys Mallory was the external author brought in to write stress-test simulations for the VMSS doctrine. She had the complete source material: the charter, the whitepaper, the governance model, the layer mechanics, the STI architecture, the economic model and the full simulation library. She read the library first, all of it, and then wrote three simulations in its exact voice and format.

        "The Flatline Resident" was about Nadia El-Amin, a Sanctuary resident with an STI of 91 who had not generated a meaningful signal in four years. Nadia read, walked, went neural diving recreationally and kept her social contact minimal. She violated nothing. In Carys's story a phasing review triggered by mismatch between static score and static life classified her inertness as placement drift and phased her to Main Layer. Carys wrote it as frictionless, non-punitive and administratively clean, and it was compelling.

        "The Downward Visitor" followed Tomas Varga, a Main Layer entrepreneur who made weekly trips to -2. He bought materials and labor at lower-layer prices, manufactured finished goods and sold them in Main at upper-layer margins. The story explored bidirectional public signal conflict, with Main endorsing his competence while -2 viewed him as extractive. Its prose was the strongest of the three.

        "The Structural Petition That Almost Slipped" traced a -1 petition to reduce the STI threshold for -1 reassignment from 70 to 60. It followed the petition through Article XXVIII processing, the Meritboard's recognition of it as structural and the deferral to Article XI, and the jurisdictional drama was well constructed.

        The prose in all three was fluent and the vocabulary precise, and each story's structure mirrored the library's entries exactly. Her partner, Jonah, read them at the kitchen table. He knew nothing about the doctrine beyond what the stories told him.

        "What's wrong with them?" she asked.

        "Nothing I can see." He turned back to the Varga story. "I'd read this one twice."

        All three came back rejected on mechanical review.

        The note on "The Flatline Resident" began The mechanic is fabricated. The doctrine did not treat Sanctuary as a performance environment or impose activity quotas. An STI flatlined on inactivity and did not trigger removal, and a resident with an STI of 91 who violated nothing met every stated requirement for Sanctuary residency. Carys had treated Sanctuary like a corporate office with quarterly reviews, invented a review mechanism that did not exist, and punished a woman for living quietly in a layer designed for people who had earned the right to live however they chose. The doctrine said the system did not penalize withdrawal, and her story had penalized it and called it administrative. She searched the source material for placement drift and got nothing back.

        The note on "The Downward Visitor" was shorter. The mechanic collapses on the first transaction. Currency siloing, a foundational economic feature codified in the charter, the whitepaper and the systems page, made layer currencies non-convertible across layer boundaries, and the cross-layer arbitrage model the whole story was built on was structurally impossible. She had matched the vocabulary of economic architecture, the note said, and missed the single mechanic that defines cross-layer economic interaction.

        Jonah leaned over to read it. "So he can't buy his materials with -2 money and sell the goods for Main money?"

        "No," Carys said.

        The note on the petition said The premise is wrong. The STI range of 70–84 for Main Layer was a descriptive population average, the typical behavioral profile of Main Layer residents. It was not a reassignment trigger threshold. Reassignment was driven by qualifying behavioral breaches evaluated through multi-factor review under Article XII, and no STI score crossing a numeric line drove it. Her petition asked to change a number that did not function the way her story assumed. The whole jurisdictional conflict, regulatory against structural, the Meritboard's identification and the Article XI deferral, was built on a misread of what STI ranges represent.

        That night Jonah read "The Downward Visitor" again.

        "Still good?" she asked.

        "Still good."

        Key lesson: Fluent prose hides a wrong premise from readers who judge by how it sounds. The reliable check tests every assumption against the published rules.

        There were eleven of them, all residents of +1 Sanctuary: former legal scholars, policy architects and institutional strategists, with STIs ranging from 87 to 94. Hanne Ostrova had been a legal scholar, so the drafting fell to her. Their grievance was political. They opposed the 10:1 penalty-to-reward asymmetry in STI scoring, which they believed suppressed ambitious contribution by making caution the only rational behavioral strategy. They had raised it through every available channel, and the answer had been the same every time: the asymmetry was intended design, documented across the charter, the whitepaper and the systems page, and it optimized against harmful variance.

        When the standard channels were exhausted, they believed they had found a structural vulnerability in VMSS governance. Corvin Tate, a policy architect, laid it out one evening in Hanne's rooms.

        "We take it to the Supreme Court," he said.

        "They won't hear it," Hanne said. "Article XXI gives the Court edge cases, where automated governance and existing doctrine come out ambiguous. Ours gets the same answer every time."

        "And a ruling from them becomes settled precedent. That ends it for good. So we skip the standard petition and frame the grievance as a constitutional novelty."

        Hanne's first petition argued that the asymmetry created a structural tension with Article V's rights provisions. A system designed to protect individual flourishing, she wrote, could not simultaneously impose a penalty structure that made risk-averse withdrawal the dominant strategy. She framed it as two charter principles in collision: the right to flourish against a scoring architecture that penalized the behavior flourishing requires.

        The AI governance novelty filter evaluated the petition against the existing doctrine corpus, and the rejection listed three deterministic answers already on record. The whitepaper's STI architecture section explicitly documented the asymmetry as intended design, with a stated rationale. The systems page listed penalty asymmetry as a governance principle. The charter's Article XII multi-factor evaluation framework prevented any single metric, penalty accumulation included, from unilaterally determining layer assignment. The filter found a policy disagreement dressed in constitutional language, with nothing novel in it, and applied existing doctrine.

        For the second petition Hanne left the asymmetry out entirely. It argued that Sanctuary was in contribution stagnation: the STI architecture's penalty weighting had produced a population of high-scoring residents who held their position through inactivity rather than contribution, and that stagnation was a systemic failure the Court should address. It cited no charter article. It described an emergent condition that existing doctrine did not anticipate.

        This rejection gave two deterministic answers. First, the doctrine explicitly stated that STI flatlines on inactivity: withdrawal generated no signals and did not trigger removal. Sanctuary was not a performance environment, it had no activity quotas, and a resident who violated nothing and met the 85+ STI requirement stayed. Second, the petition's premise, that inactivity was a systemic failure, contradicted the documented design intent of Sanctuary as a layer earned through sustained conduct, not sustained output.

        Hanne looked the category up in the doctrine corpus. It now held two rejection records.

        The third petition was their most sophisticated. Corvin drew it on the wall of Hanne's rooms as a circle. In it, a Sanctuary resident entered a SAD, was excluded for failing the domain metric, returned to Sanctuary and re-entered the same SAD, and could theoretically cycle indefinitely. Article VII's layer mobility provisions and Article IX's SAD exclusion mechanics, the petition argued, created an undocumented interaction, a mobility loop the charter did not explicitly address, and that made it genuine constitutional novelty requiring Court interpretation.

        The filter evaluated the scenario mechanically. SAD exclusion under Article IX meant automatic return to the layer below, with loss of domain access and no layer reassignment. Re-entry to the same SAD required meeting the domain's metric gate again, and the resident met the gate or did not. There was no undocumented interaction. The filter found no ambiguity, no competing provisions and no outcome the existing doctrine failed to resolve, and rejected the petition. The category now held three rejection records.

        Hanne looked at the circle for a long time. Each time round, the same deterministic rule was applied again. Corvin wiped it off the wall that night.

        They tried one final approach. The fourth petition argued that the novelty filter itself, the automated gating mechanism that had rejected all three, lacked explicit charter authorization. Article XXI established the Court and defined its jurisdiction but did not, in their reading, explicitly authorize an AI system to determine what reached the Court. Hanne framed it as a separation-of-powers question: should the system that enforces doctrine also decide which challenges to doctrine are heard?

        The filter evaluated the petition against Article XXI's own text and quoted it in the rejection. Hanne read the sentence out to the room.

        "Access to the Court is gated by an automated novelty filter administered by the AI governance system — not by the Court itself."

        "That's the charter," someone said.

        "Article XXI." Hanne had cited it on the petition's first page.

        The rejection gave the reason the filter sat with AI governance: so the Court could not control its own docket. It listed, under their category, the three earlier records the petition had been evaluated against. It went into the corpus as the fourth record. No future petition from any citizen could reach the Court through any of those four angles, and the faction's whole category of grievance, penalty asymmetry reframed through four different constitutional lenses, was settled precedent.

        They had spent months on four increasingly sophisticated framings. They had expected a court like the ones they knew from historical study, where persistence, clever framing and procedural creativity eventually found a sympathetic ear or an exploitable gap.

        "Is there a fifth angle?" Corvin asked.

        Hanne closed the corpus without answering.

        Key lesson: The novelty filter asks only whether existing doctrine already answers a question, however it is framed. Each rejection narrows what can still reach the Court.

        Mirela Saar, who taught ethics in Sanctuary, watched the speech twice. It came from the president of one of VMSS's earliest treaty partners, an allied nation of 1.2 billion that operated a four-ring gradient model. Through its own charter process, with broad popular support, the ally had passed a constitutional amendment that removed the implant kill switch from its military doctrine and decommissioned its nanobot neutralization plume capability.

        The line the feeds kept replaying came first: A civilization that can kill its citizens with a thought is not a civilization that trusts them. The president went on to say that the ally was removing the instruments of instantaneous death from its governance architecture, because it believed consequence could be delivered without the capacity for remote execution. The speech was well crafted and deliberately comparative. Without a word against VMSS, it presented the ally as the version of gradient governance that had chosen trust over lethality, and it circulated widely across allied media networks and within VMSS itself.

        The majority of VMSS, people whose daily lives had never intersected with the kill switch or the plumes, registered it as foreign policy and moved on. A smaller population took it seriously, and Mirela was part of it. Her friend Pavel was not.

        "It's their military," he said over dinner. "Why does it bother you?"

        "It's in my implant, Pavel. It's in yours."

        "Has it ever done anything to you?"

        "No," she said.

        It never would. The kill switch was a classified military capability, housed in hardware she could not access and activated by an authority chain she would never trigger. She had objected to it before the speech, and done nothing about it.

        Over the following eighteen months approximately 4,200 VMSS citizens emigrated to the ally. The channel was already open, since free movement between allied civilizations was established doctrine, and Mirela used a right she already had. She left under the standard Article X exit protocol, which always permitted exit from Main Layer and above. Nobody obstructed her or put her through enhanced screening, and the officer at the border did not ask why she was going.

        She told him anyway. "The kill switch. I object to it on principle."

        He entered it and waved her through.

        Her behavioral record went with her intact. At the allied border it went through a ledger audit, and she entered the four-ring system at the layer her conduct warranted, the ally's highest tier. Most of the emigrants entered there.

        When the eighteen months were up, Pavel forwarded her a VMSS news summary of the wave. Above it he had written one line: You're a rounding error.

        4,200 emigrants of 4.3 billion: 0.0001%. Overwhelmingly Sanctuary and upper Main Layer. Average STI 88. Disproportionately concentrated in philosophy, ethics, political theory and legal scholarship. From -1 or below: zero. STI under 75: zero. From any layer where the kill switch serves as active deterrent: zero. Engineers, fabrication specialists, medical researchers, infrastructure operators or military-adjacent professionals: zero. Leaving over safety, abundance, medical access, pre-intervention, institutional trust, economic opportunity or quality of life: zero. Reason given at the border, every case: philosophical objection to the state's capacity for remote lethality. Reporting that it threatened them or affected their daily lives: none.

        In the ally the 4,200 integrated smoothly. They were high-trust immigrants with strong philosophical credentials and no security complications, by every measure a net positive for the ally in the short term. Their behavioral records were clean, and their STI equivalents mapped to the ally's highest tier without friction. Several of them, Mirela among them, published influential work on the ethics of governance lethality, which the ally's domestic intellectual culture absorbed enthusiastically. The president cited the immigration wave as validation of the amendment.

        In her second year Mirela shared a panel on governance lethality with Aurek Dahl, a retired colonel of the ally's forces. His first slide had two lines on it: Kill switch: implanted threats, instantaneous, any scale, zero collateral damage. and Nanobot plumes: non-implanted threats, near-instant lethality, precise targeting.

        "We decommissioned both," he said, "and replaced neither."

        "They closed the evasion loop between them," Mirela said. "Take your implant out to escape the switch, and you're inside the plumes' envelope."

        "And we've opened it. A man here who removes his implant is outside remote neutralization entirely. We answer him with physical interception, law enforcement engagement and kinetic military action, for every threat VMSS handles with a signal."

        He moved to his next slide: Universal implant infrastructure: monitors everything, stops nothing remotely. Terminal override: none. "Every citizen still carries a device the military could once access in extremis," he said. "The switch was the failsafe that made universal deployment militarily coherent."

        She said it was more humane. He said it was a vulnerability, and the moderator moved them on.

        Within three years VMSS's layer equivalence mapping, which evaluated allied citizens at the border, began to register divergence. The ally's behavioral thresholds had softened, not dramatically but measurably. Without the terminal deterrent backstopping enforcement, its governance had shifted posture, and Mirela could see it from her own district. Penalties were lighter and response times longer. Conduct VMSS classified as qualifying behavioral breaches went to remediation programs instead of layer reassignment. The ally's population approved, her neighbors among them, and its internal metrics showed satisfaction rising.

        Benedek Hal, who had the office next to hers, was turned back at the VMSS border that spring. The ledger audit found that his home-system classification no longer met VMSS's threshold, and he came home in good standing. Entry denial rates for allied citizens climbed from 2% to 7% over three years, and a citizen the ally considered top-tier now had a behavioral record that VMSS read as Main Layer at best. When the ally's diplomatic corps raised the issue, VMSS's reply was mechanical. The border applied published thresholds. Where the ally's classification diverged from VMSS's, the citizen returned home in good standing. VMSS did not adjust its thresholds to accommodate drift, and the ally was free to tighten its own or accept that its citizens would face increasing entry restrictions. The reply carried no directive and no ultimatum.

        In year five an organized criminal network operating within the ally's -2 equivalent tier coordinated a mass implant removal. Over six weeks, thirty-one individuals disabled or extracted their implants. In VMSS that would have put them inside the nanobot plume's operational envelope, identified, tracked and neutralizable within hours. In the ally they were thirty-one untracked actors, entirely outside the governance system's detection architecture.

        Conventional law enforcement spent fourteen weeks locating and apprehending twenty-six of them. Five remained unaccounted for. During those fourteen weeks the network carried out armed robberies across three districts, Mirela's among them. Eleven civilians died, and there was significant property destruction. The pharmacy on her corner burned in the ninth week.

        The ally's media called it a law enforcement failure. Dahl went on the evening news to say that conventional law enforcement had never been designed to locate and neutralize actors who had deliberately exited the surveillance architecture.

        "The kill switch and the plumes were," he said.

        Mirela looked for a statement from VMSS and found none. It did not comment publicly on the incident, issued nothing about the amendment, and did not press the ally to reinstate the decommissioned capabilities. The alliance treaty required mutual defense and structural compatibility, and nothing in it required identical military doctrine. The ally remained a treaty partner, and its citizens went on meeting increasing entry restrictions at the VMSS border.

        Like several of the 4,200, she wrote an analysis of the incident. Eleven deaths, she argued, did not justify reinstating remote lethality over a billion citizens. It was a coherent argument. VMSS did not engage with it.

        None of the 4,200 went back. They were settled, integrated and still committed to their philosophical position. The pharmacy on Mirela's corner reopened in the autumn behind a new steel shutter.

        Key lesson: Few citizens leave over a weapon almost none of them will ever meet. Its absence is felt in the rare case it was built for.

        Odile Ferrand had sat in a parliament before she emigrated, and she was one of the founders who signed the sovereign treaty that created the new civilization on a contested landmass. It opened with 380 million people, and the projections said 900 million within a century. The founders were political theorists, constitutional scholars and former parliamentarians like her, who had emigrated from democratic nations. They took the VMSS stack whole, from the implants, the STI ledger and the AI governance system to the mega-walls, the backup vessels, the kill switch and the nanobot plumes. The layers, the technology and the consequence model were identical. The charter changed one thing: each layer would elect its own government, through political parties.

        Early on, the VMSS alliance admitted them as a treaty partner. The five layers mapped one to one, and when Odile first crossed into VMSS, the border read her and let her through. The VMSS official who met her on the other side, Arvo Keld, asked her why they wanted parties.

        "Your Meritboard is chosen on measurable achievement, and your President is drawn from it," she said. "They answer to competence metrics. The people they govern never get a vote on them."

        "And yours?"

        "Ours will answer to voters. We're calling it Democratic Gradient Governance."

        At home she stood for a Main Layer seat. Each layer elected its own parliament by universal suffrage, one representative for every million residents in districts drawn the way VMSS drew them, and elections came every four years. Odile's party won in Main, and the parliament's majority elected her Prime Minister, the layer's chief executive. That gave her a seat on the Federal Council, which governed cross-layer federal policy, with the Prime Ministers of the other four layers. The five of them elected a President from among themselves by majority vote, and they confirmed the Supreme Court justices the President nominated. In VMSS the justices came from the Meritboard's legal-interpretation ranking. Below the parliaments nothing had changed: the implants recorded behavioral data, the ledger tracked trust, and the AI delivered consequence automatically.

        The first electoral cycle produced competent governments in every layer, run by high-caliber officials. The founding population had chosen to come, and it ran disproportionately to the motivated and the idealistic, people invested in the project's success. Sanctuary's parliament filled with philosophers, scientists and institutional designers who would have qualified for any Meritboard. Odile's benches held entrepreneurs, engineers and civic leaders. Even -1's parliament, smaller and rougher, sent representatives with genuine knowledge of the people they served. The parliaments worked on regulatory law, zoning, resource allocation, public infrastructure and cultural programming, while the AI delivered consequence without interference. Citizens moved freely between the two civilizations. Odile cited the early figures in every speech as validation, and when Arvo told her at the five-year mark that the figures were indistinguishable from a well-run VMSS founding, she cited that too.

        The second campaign brought the model's first structural tension. Her strategist, Corvin Halloran, asked what she was running on.

        "It works," she said. "All of it."

        "The other side is promising to change things. What are you going to change?"

        "Nothing's broken."

        "Then find what people are unhappy about."

        Her platform was economic populism: tax adjustments, UBI supplements, PJS expansion and regulatory relief for particular industries. She won, and her parliament passed a series of industry-friendly regulations at 52%, a simple majority on party-line votes. In VMSS, citizens would have petitioned, experts would have drafted, and the population would have had to ratify at 80%. None of these regulations would have got there. The AI ignored the campaign and enforced the charter.

        In Sanctuary the parties ran philosophical refinement campaigns over cultural policy, arts funding, neural diving regulation and SAD governance. The stakes were low, the voters trusted one another, and the campaigns had substance. In -1 every candidate's platform came down to the same promise, and -1's Prime Minister, Bram Ostrow, said it aloud once, at the Council table: "I will make it easier to get back to Main." His voters wanted ascent more than anything. His parties competed on promises to advocate for relaxed behavioral thresholds, faster remediation timelines and expanded pathways to Main.

        "Does anyone down there run on the system working?" Odile asked him once.

        "On telling voters the system works and -1 is the intended consequence of their conduct?" Ostrow said. "That loses every election."

        He couldn't touch the AI's enforcement thresholds, which were charter-level. His parliament could pass regulatory law, and it did: bureaucratic friction around enforcement, review processes that delayed consequence delivery, and funding for legal advocacy programs that contested STI evaluations through every available channel.

        Before the third campaign Corvin had a different question. "What have you built that a voter can point to?"

        She drafted the Contextual Review Boards herself, and her parliament passed them. They were elected panels that reviewed flagged STI evaluations and sent the AI non-binding recommendations. She worded the regulation carefully, so the boards advised and never overrode, and nothing in it contradicted the charter. On the campaign stage she said, "I established a process that gives citizens a voice in the system that governs them." The hall stood for her.

        The first citizen whose evaluation a board flagged went into a queue and waited while the board met. The AI read the recommendation, found it non-binding, and delivered the original consequence, later than it would have arrived without the board.

        After the fourth election, -1 had chosen leniency candidates three cycles running. Its remediation programs were funded lavishly, and its legal advocacy had become an institution. Every behavioral breach drew an automatic review request, filed by the resident's own elected representative. The AI processed each one and delivered the consequence anyway, and processing time had tripled. Ostrow brought the figure to the Council.

        "Tripled," he said. "My people finally feel heard."

        -2's Prime Minister spoke for people reassigned for predatory violence. Her layer's representatives campaigned on survival infrastructure: medical access, economic development, personal safety inside the layer. Those were legitimate governance concerns, and -2's two parties channeled them through ideology. One argued -2 was too harsh and wanted expanded institutional presence. The other wanted -2 more autonomous, with reduced federal oversight. Odile read both platforms. Neither mentioned what -2's residents had done.

        Between the fifteenth and twentieth years the Council split, and federal enforcement policy deadlocked. The Sanctuary Prime Minister's voters wanted the system preserved, and Ostrow's wanted it softened. Both had been elected and both sat at the same table, so every federal policy discussion became a negotiation between a population that had earned its position and one placed there by consequence. In VMSS, -1 petitioned like every other layer, and no elected advocate at the federal level had a career in making it more comfortable. Here, on enforcement, Ostrow and -2's Prime Minister voted as a bloc, and -3's Prime Minister, who spoke for the terminal layer, joined them when it suited him. That made three of the five votes, all from punitive layers. Odile and the Sanctuary Prime Minister were outvoted on enforcement-adjacent policy.

        The bloc couldn't override the charter, which the AI enforced whatever the Council voted. It could block federal regulatory proposals, defund cross-layer enforcement coordination and direct federal resources to remediation infrastructure in the lower layers, effectively subsidizing consequence mitigation there with upper-layer tax revenue.

        "That's Main's tax money," Odile said after one vote.

        "It's federal money," Ostrow said. "We voted."

        The Supreme Court stepped in twice. It struck down a -1 regulation that effectively created a shadow appeals process for layer reassignment, and it struck down a Council budget allocation that redirected enforcement funding to legal advocacy. Both rulings were correct on the charter, and both were politically catastrophic. The President had nominated those justices and the Council, Odile among its members, had confirmed them, and now they were striking down the Council's own policies. Ostrow campaigned for re-election on judicial reform.

        In the twentieth year the alliance conducted its routine border calibration review, and Odile read the report at her kitchen table the night it arrived. The findings were stark. The first page said her civilization's STI distribution had shifted measurably, and the lines under it said where:

        Main Layer: average STI drifting downward. Cause: procedural delays let borderline conduct accumulate before consequence arrives. Citizens not behaving worse.
        -1: population grown disproportionately. Remediation has made the layer more habitable and reduced the urgency of behavioral correction. Ascent not accelerated.
        Sanctuary: population plateaued. Pathway unchanged on paper; cultural signal shifted. Same sustained non-harmful conduct required, amid comment periods, review boards and advisory recommendations in place of zero-latency consequence.
        Border entry denials, citizens entering VMSS: 3% rising to 11% over twenty years.

        Beside each finding the report printed VMSS's own at the same twenty-year mark. VMSS showed no comparable drift. Its laws still came from petitions on genuine regulatory gaps its population had identified, ratified at 80%, and its AI delivered consequence at the same latency as on its first day. A note on the last page called her civilization's drift passive and persistent, like the Softer Ring's, and entirely produced by its own governance choices.

        She called Arvo in the morning.

        "What does VMSS want us to do?"

        "Nothing," he said. "The thresholds are the ones you crossed the first time."

        Her citizens were fed, housed, safe and governed. The layers were intact, the walls stood, the kill switch and the plumes still worked, and the AI still enforced the charter. By any Earth-historical standard it was an extraordinary success: a five-layer gradient civilization with universal abundance, functional consequence delivery and democratic representation at every level. That afternoon Corvin sent over the first draft of the next platform.

        Key lesson: Elected officials need discretionary authority to justify their office, and VMSS is built to keep discretion out of consequence. Below Sanctuary, every constituency wants that discretion used for leniency.

        Footnote — What the Honeymoon Proves: The first electoral cycle produced competent, high-caliber governments in every layer, so merit-based selection is not the only way to produce competent leaders. Elections can produce excellent leaders, just not consistently across generations. The first generation is self-selected for idealism, the second inherits party infrastructure, and the third inherits incumbency. The Meritboard selects on fixed criteria, measurable achievement, so it does not degrade from one generation to the next.

        Hanne Vell was reassigned to -1 for fraud in the civilization's third year. It was an allied civilization, founded twelve years after VMSS, with 1.6 billion people in five rings, and it ran the full VMSS architecture with one structural change. The cover of her arrival packet called that change the humane completion of gradient governance: people change, and the Recovery Gradient asked them to prove it. It offered earned redemption, to a standard higher than original placement had ever required. The first page set it out. A -1 resident who kept an STI above 78 for fifteen continuous years, with zero qualifying breaches, became eligible for review for recovery to Main Layer. The AI governance system ran the review as a multi-factor evaluation: STI trajectory, behavioral consistency, peer-signal analysis, and a victim-impact assessment that gave the original victim formal input. Meeting the threshold earned a review, and the review could still deny recovery. Below that, in smaller type: -2: STI above 72 for twenty-five years, zero qualifying breaches, eligible for review for recovery to -1. -3: terminal, no pathway.

        The man who came down on the same transport, Rudi Selk, had been reassigned for a DUI at thirty. He read the page on the platform.

        "In VMSS this would be for good," he said. "Backup vessels, augmentation, you're effectively immortal. That's three hundred years for one DUI at thirty." He folded the page into his jacket. "Here it's fifteen, if you can prove it."

        Every quarter the bulletin in Hanne's stairwell reported STI distributions, population flows, enforcement latency and behavioral metrics indistinguishable from VMSS's. One issue carried the alliance's notice admitting the civilization as a treaty partner with clean border protocol, and the result of VMSS's layer equivalence mapping: no calibration issues. Hanne read each issue on her way to work and saw nothing in it to suggest the deterrent had weakened. The fifteen- and twenty-five-year minimums had not run, and through the civilization's first decade the count of residents qualified for review stayed at zero.

        Hanne kept her STI above 78 and her behavior impeccable by every metric. She checked her score every morning and rented her flat by the month. In fifteen years she never signed a longer lease.

        The first cohort became eligible in the fifteenth year, and the results went up in the canteen. Of the roughly 3.2 million residents who had entered -1 in the civilization's first two years, about 340,000, just over 10%, had met the STI and zero-breach criteria. Of those, 220,000 passed the review. Victims had blocked about 40,000, and the other 80,000 were denied on behavioral trajectory or peer-signal grounds despite meeting the STI threshold. The 220,000 recoveries were processed over six months. The woman across Hanne's landing was one of them, and she left with one bag.

        Within two years of the first recoveries, Main's qualifying breach rate rose 8%. It was no crime wave. The young man who moved into the empty flat across the landing was no hardened offender. He had run the calculation first.

        "Fifteen years," he told Hanne. "Not the rest of my life."

        The recoveries split -1 in two. Residents on the clock, like Hanne, kept their scores high, avoided every risk and optimized for the fifteen years. The rest had failed the criteria or never meant to pursue them, and now they lived beside neighbors with something to lose. Hanne's bicycle was stolen that spring, and the AI recorded the theft and processed it through standard enforcement like any other. A man from the ground floor took to stopping her on the stairs and raising his voice, to see whether she would raise hers in public. Later he asked to be paid to stop.

        Rudi had held 79 for fourteen years and eleven months. In month 179 the same man followed him into the canteen, and Rudi turned on him, and the argument got loud enough that the canteen went quiet. It wasn't a qualifying breach, only a conflict the STI could see. Rudi's score dropped to 77, and his fifteen-year clock reset. Hanne had wanted to shout at that man for two years. She never had.

        Her own fifteen years ended the next month. The review approved her. She entered Main Layer with a clean layer status and a fresh economic position: no asset restoration, a rebuild from the UBI baseline. The AI would not weight the notation on her STI ledger in any future evaluation, and the population could weight it however it chose. The notation was permanent and public, a factual record of prior -1 residency and recovery.

        Work moved her twice in her first years in Main, and the second move put her in the same district as Mirela Stoica, whose savings she had taken. The AI placed no restriction on where recovered residents settled. Mirela read the notation in the market before she recognized Hanne's face.

        "They wrote to me," Mirela said. "I hadn't thought about you in fifteen years. Then a letter told me you were eligible to come up to Main and asked for my input."

        "I'm sorry," Hanne said.

        "It was a very respectful letter." Mirela put the pears back on the stall and walked away.

        In the twenty-fifth year the first -2 cohort became eligible. They had been reassigned for predatory violence: assault, sexual assault and attempted murder. -2 was populated by people whose qualifying breach had involved direct physical harm to another person's body. The review was rigorous and the standard genuinely high. Their victims opposed recovery at nearly three times the rate of victims of -1-level offenses, and the AI denied the vast majority on behavioral trajectory grounds. Fewer than 4% of the eligible cohort passed, fewer than 8,000 people in a civilization of 1.6 billion. In -2 people began to organize their lives around the twenty-five-year clock, as -1 had around the fifteen. Rudi wrote that two of the recovered had taken flats in his building. He was partway through his second fifteen years.

        By the thirtieth year the re-contact notices had produced a formal protest movement in Main. Hanne met its march in her own district on the day the alliance published its thirty-year border calibration review, and the market screens carried the figures. Main's qualifying breach rate was 14% higher than VMSS's, all of it attributable to marginal deterrent erosion. -1 had split into recovery-track and non-recovery populations, with measurably different social structures and conflict patterns, and -2 had begun the same split. The civilization was measurably softer than VMSS on the one metric VMSS considers non-negotiable, the permanence of consequence for qualifying harm. Its calibration drift was slower than the Softer Ring's or the Elected Ring's, because the thresholds were demanding, but it ran in the same direction, and its border entry denial rate at VMSS had risen to 9%.

        None of these costs was catastrophic. At the same mark VMSS showed none of them: no deterrent erosion, no victim re-contact, no recovery-track population and no STI gaming. There, a punitive layer's STI decided a resident's district, contracts and standing among peers but never whether they left, and a -1 resident who had lived quietly for thirty years had a home in -1.

        The marchers carried a banner that read PERMANENCE IS A VICTIM'S RIGHT, NOT JUST A PUNISHMENT. Mirela was holding one end of it. Hanne waited on the curb until the march had passed, then crossed the street behind it.

        Key lesson: A way back out of a punitive layer changes how its residents live there and brings their victims back into the case. A behavior score cannot certify a changed character.

        Footnote — The Immortality Argument: The Recovery Gradient is the most defensible modification any allied civilization has attempted. Its thresholds are demanding, its review is rigorous, and its victim-input mechanism is structurally unprecedented. Its designers genuinely understood the architecture they were modifying, and they believed that permanence, applied to an immortal population, is a disproportionate consequence for a single qualifying act. Theirs is the one objection to permanence that carries genuine philosophical weight over centuries: with backup vessels and effective immortality, a DUI at age thirty determines a person's civilizational environment for the next three hundred years. The argument has moral weight. VMSS's counter, that the system does not guess, it knows, is mechanically sound. But whether a three-century consequence for a single qualifying act stays proportional over an immortal lifespan is a question the traditional model answers with doctrine rather than data, because it has never needed to produce data. The Recovery Gradient is the only allied nation generating that data. If its 10% recovery rate and demanding thresholds eventually produce a stable equilibrium without deterrent erosion, a possibility the thirty-year window does not confirm or exclude, the argument for permanence may need to be defended on grounds beyond "it works because we never tried the alternative."

        Ysolde Kaarn signed the calibration table the week her division announced the breakthrough. She was an engineer in the fabrication research division of a VMSS-adjacent nation, a sovereign civilization outside the alliance that ran a five-ring gradient model under its own charter. The division had built a backup vessel revival architecture that eliminated revival failure entirely. Every revival succeeded, and no death was permanent in any layer where the backup vessel link was maintained. VMSS's model was binary, full fidelity or failure. The division's model replaced the binary with a spectrum: the person who woke was never a perfect copy, and the continuity drift between the person who died and the person who woke could be measured. The table graduated it by layer, following the civilization's own institutional gradient:

        Sanctuary, Main: approx. 99.97% fidelity
        -1: approx. 0.5% drift
        -2: approx. 2% drift
        -3: approx. 8% drift

        At the announcement the division's director set it against VMSS's numbers. "In VMSS, one revival in a thousand fails in -2," she said. "Across millions of people and centuries, that is hundreds of thousands of permanent deaths from a technological limitation rather than a policy choice. Our citizens always come back, in every layer." Drift was the cost, she said, and it was graduated: nearly imperceptible in the upper layers, where institutional investment was highest, and more pronounced in the lower layers, where the consequence architecture was designed to be harsher. The layers that had earned the most institutional trust received the most precise continuity, and the layers the civilization had withdrawn from received less fidelity. Unlike VMSS, her nation kept backup vessel infrastructure running in -3, where Ysolde's brother Tavi lived.

        In Main, where Ysolde lived, the effect was almost entirely positive. Under the binary model VMSS still ran, every upper-layer revival carried a one-in-a-million chance of failing, statistically trivial each time. A citizen who expected to live for centuries would, statistically, die and be revived dozens of times, and the cumulative chance became personally meaningful. Revival anxiety, the low-grade awareness that any death carried a small but non-zero chance of being final, went away. That year's surveys had upper-layer citizens reporting higher satisfaction, lower revival anxiety and a negligible difference in continuity. Her partner Soren drowned sailing in the second year and was revived at 99.97% fidelity. He remembered everything, recognized everyone and kept every skill. The division called the missing 0.03% emotional rounding: slight attenuations in how intensely particular memories felt, minor shifts in how he responded to particular things. Most revived people in the upper layers could not identify what had changed. Months later Ysolde told the story of the night they met, which had always made him laugh, and he smiled and said, "I remember." She noticed a flatness in it she could not have named.

        In -1 the drift was noticeable. The division's field interviews came back with the residents' own words for it. "The first two were clean," one man said. "The third was soft. My wife says she can see the drift marks." Revived -1 residents kept their full factual memory, their skills and their identity. They lost something in personality texture: reduced emotional granularity, slight shifts in how they responded to stress, occasional gaps in contextual memory, like forgetting why they had walked into a room. It was noticeable to them and to their intimates, and it disabled no one. Each revival compounded the drift cumulatively, not additively, and a resident revived three times carried about 1.5%, measurably different in emotional texture, stress response and affective depth from the person who had entered -1. Residents called the slow attenuation across revivals thinning.

        In -2 the effect was transformative. Being revived twice was common there, because predatory violence produced high mortality, and at 2% per revival that came to about 4%. Factual memory stayed largely intact while emotional memory degraded meaningfully, including their emotional memory of the violence they had committed, of the relationships they had before reassignment and of who they had been on entering -2. Relationships felt thinner. People knew whom they loved and felt it less. Several reported something the researchers had not predicted, reduced remorse. Their remorse was not gone. They knew what they had done and felt less about it. The charter committee reviewed the finding and classified it as an acceptable trade-off. Under traditional technology, its ruling noted, the alternative was permanent death for 1 in 1,000 -2 revivals.

        Tavi's first revival in -3 brought back a recognizably similar, emotionally attenuated version of him. His second brought the cumulative drift to about 15%, and his third to close to 22%. He still had his name, his language, his skills and every fact of his autobiography, and he had lost nearly a quarter of what the division's papers called his emotional architecture. He was not brain-damaged; his cognition tested intact. When Ysolde came through his door he looked up and knew her face, and the feeling of recognizing it did not come. He knew she was his sister. He remembered loving their mother the way he remembered her address.

        After a decade -3 had a visible demographic of high-revival residents, unprecedented in the VMSS model family: people technically continuous with their original selves who were functionally different people. Their skills worked and their language was fluent. What had degraded was the connective tissue of personality, the emotional responses, affective patterns and experiential depth that made a person feel like themselves to the people around them. By then Tavi had been revived five times and was approaching 35% cumulative drift, and the people in his block called residents like him present but hollow.

        The charter committee began to debate whether high-drift revival was meaningful continuity or a different form of death, one where the body survived and the person did not. The woman downstairs from Tavi had been revived once and still felt like herself, and lived like it. The man who fixed Tavi's door noticed his own changes and kept a notebook of things he used to like; some weeks it helped, and lately he got lost more often between what he remembered of himself and what he felt. Tavi was past that. He remembered being someone else and could not reach the experience of having been him.

        Delegations came to the division through those years, from across the alliance and beyond. The alliance nations ran traditional backup vessels, and their evaluators did not dismiss the drift vessel; zero revival failure was a genuine advance. A delegate from one of their Sanctuary layers did the arithmetic across the table for Ysolde. He expected forty revivals over three centuries, each with a one-in-a-million chance of failing.

        "That starts to be about me," he said.

        For upper layers the question was whether near-zero drift was an acceptable trade for guaranteed revival, and the answer was genuinely competitive. At 0.03% drift the drift vessel removed the statistically rare but psychologically corrosive possibility of permanent death in the safest environment, with no meaningful continuity concern, and for upper layers that was the better outcome. Multiple alliance nations opened formal technology-sharing negotiations to deploy it in their upper layers while retaining binary revival for their lower layers. Below Main, their consensus ran, the calculus reversed. Binary revival with elevated failure rates preserved perfect fidelity for everyone who survived the process, and they preferred it to guaranteed survival with cumulative personality erosion, particularly in -2 and -3, where high mortality and multiple revival cycles would compound drift into a de facto identity replacement program.

        The non-allied delegations came separately. Their nations had no layered consequence architecture and no punitive lower layers where drift compounded across violent deaths, so the offer was straightforward: revival that never failed, with negligible drift, at the standard of care their whole population would receive. Several opened direct bilateral negotiations with Ysolde's government, bypassing the alliance entirely. Her nation became the sole source of a capability every civilization wanted and the alliance's framework did not control, and the drift vessel became a diplomatic asset.

        VMSS's Meritboard commissioned its own formal review, the most measured of any, and its reviewer spent a week in the -3 installation with Ysolde. His findings were that in Sanctuary and Main the drift vessel was superior to binary revival by every metric that mattered: zero failure, imperceptible drift, the end of cumulative revival anxiety across immortal lifespans. In -1 and -2 it was inferior by the single metric that defined those layers, the integrity of the person who came back. VMSS's foundational promise was continuity, not innocence, and by continuity it meant the complete person, not the survival of a body carrying their memories. On his last day he stood with Ysolde outside Tavi's block.

        "We'd have no framework for him," he said. "On paper he's the same citizen."

        By the twentieth year -3 had produced a generation of high-drift residents, people with five, eight, twelve revivals and cumulative drift above 40%. They were ambulatory, functional, employable and legally continuous with their original selves. They remembered their lives and did not experience them. The charter committee formally ruled that drift revival constituted meaningful continuity at every level: whoever woke, at any cumulative drift, was the same person who died. The ruling was legally necessary. Without it, the charter had no category for people who were simultaneously alive and no longer themselves. VMSS observed. Its Meritboard review concluded internally that both revival models were honest trade-offs, and VMSS made no public comment.

        The ruling did not resolve what the families lived with. That year an account Tavi had written circulated widely. He had 44% cumulative drift, and he described it as "living in the memory of a life that happened to someone who was almost me." Ysolde read it on the tram.

        She went down that weekend. He was glad to see her, and said so at the right moment. He poured the tea the way their father used to, holding the pot high, and she watched him remember to do it. When she mentioned Soren he asked after him by name and waited, correctly, for the answer.

        "I know I've drifted," he said. "I remember being more. I can't get at what it felt like."

        She drank the tea. He held the pot high again for the second cup.

        Key lesson: Each revival model publishes its cost, a small chance of permanent death or a loss with every revival. The drift cost builds up in the person who survives, so it weighs most in layers where people die often.

        Footnote — The Hybrid Path: The simulation identifies the hybrid approach, drift vessels for the upper layers and binary revival for the lower, as the likely alliance consensus. VMSS itself would logically adopt the upper-layer half, replacing its one-in-a-million failure rate with zero failure at imperceptible drift. In this case the discovering nation's innovation improves the traditional model rather than competing with it.

        Rhea Castellan voted for the recalibration. She was a neuroscientist in the Sanctuary layer of a VMSS-adjacent nation that ran a five-ring gradient model and had never been an alliance member, and she knew the change needed no new hardware. The implants already read cognition at the resolution required. VMSS kept what its implants read apart from what they shared, a deliberate constraint on a capability that existed: their high-resolution neural pattern scanning distinguished intent from mere thought, detected escalating harmful intent for pre-intervention in Sanctuary, and treated all other cognition as sovereign and non-public. They read everything and broadcast nothing, and no thought, fantasy, opinion or internal deliberation carried institutional consequence. Her nation's vote removed the software layer that did the separating, and with it the line VMSS considered foundational, between thought and action.

        The charter committee's case had been simple. A civilization that published its architecture, its enforcement rules and its citizens' behavioral records should not stop at the skull. Deception, the committee argued, was the last category of harm the system could not detect until it became action, and full cognitive transparency would eliminate it at the source, because no one could lie.

        The night it switched on, every thought became legible and every emotional state visible, and deception collapsed as the committee had anticipated. Each internal reaction, private judgment, fleeting impulse and unspoken opinion showed on the STI ledger and in the AR telemetry of anyone nearby. Rhea and her husband Jonah sat up at the kitchen table reading each other. She learned what he thought of her appearance, her habits, her family and her sexual interests, and he learned the same about her. They were still married in the morning. Her oldest friendship ended within the month, once she and her friend could each see the irritations the other had been absorbing without comment. Jonah, who composed for neural diving, settled his next contract with his studio in one sentence; each side could see the other's reservation price.

        Within months crime had dropped to near zero. Crime requires concealment, and every harmful intent now showed before it became action. Her nation had what VMSS achieved only in Sanctuary through pre-intervention: harm that never completed. VMSS's pre-intervention watched for one specific neurological pattern, escalating intent crossing a threshold from ideation to execution planning. A Sanctuary resident in VMSS could fantasize about violence without consequence, because the implant distinguished fantasy from intent. Rhea's nation displayed all cognition indiscriminately, and a citizen there could not fantasize about anything without the neighbors reading it in AR.

        Early in the first year Liesl, a postdoc in Rhea's lab, was presenting at the weekly meeting when Rhea felt a flash of rage at her over a slide out of order. It appeared on Liesl's AR display in real time. Liesl stopped talking.

        "Four hundred milliseconds," Rhea told her afterward. "I have the trace. It was never going to become anything."

        "I know," Liesl said. She sat at the other end of the table for the rest of the term.

        The lab documented the change within the first year, and it accelerated through the fifth. Researchers called it cognitive chill: the measurable suppression of spontaneous, undirected and exploratory thought in a population that knew its cognition was being observed. It showed on the lab's coding sheets. At baseline every healthy subject filled the columns for things they would never say or do: intrusive thoughts, dark humor, sexual impulses about inappropriate targets, momentary rage at loved ones, petty jealousies, fantasies of cruelty. None of it was pathological. Left alone, a healthy mind was messy, contradictory and frequently antisocial, the normal output of a brain that generates options faster than it evaluates them. Character lived in the evaluation, the decision not to act, speak or pursue, and in VMSS the implant watched only what a person did with a thought. By the third year every column held less, and the subjects were producing fewer thoughts of every kind.

        Rhea felt it in herself. Over thousands of micro-exposures her brain had learned that producing a thought had a social cost, and it was censoring itself at the neurological level. She thought in shorter loops, avoided extended internal deliberation, and carried a persistent low-grade awareness that dampened everything else. The spontaneous wandering that fed creativity, problem-solving, humor, empathy and self-knowledge began to attenuate.

        The charter committee had not modeled what came next. The messy, contradictory, socially unacceptable part of the mind that produced novel ideas was the same part that produced intrusive thoughts, dark fantasies and inappropriate impulses, and suppressing one suppressed the other. Jonah's compositions stayed technically proficient and went emotionally flat.
