He said he could no longer reach the unselfconscious state his breakthrough work came from, and the artists he knew said the same. Artistic output held its volume and lost its originality. The novels Rhea read at night had turned observational, away from the imaginative.

        Her own research slowed. A hypothesis starts with thinking absurd things provisionally, entertaining ideas that might be wrong and holding contradictory propositions at once, and now all of that was visible. At a seminar a colleague asked her, in front of the room, why she believed the model she had spent the morning trying on.

        "I don't," Rhea said. "I was trying it."

        After that she tried fewer things. Her thinking went linear and cautious and less productive, and she reached correct conclusions more slowly.

        In the tenth year the lab compared the layers, and the pattern was one the charter committee had not predicted. Rhea pinned the chart over her desk:

        Sanctuary, Main: most severe. High-trust, socially integrated, densely networked. Most exposure to others' telemetry; most to lose from a fleeting thought misread by a colleague, partner or neighbor.
        -1: moderate. Smaller population, thinner networks, lower cost to a visible thought; peers already in a consequence environment.
        -2: minimal. Residents reassigned for predatory violence, whose thoughts were already darker than average. Transparency showed what everyone there already assumed.
        -3: almost no suppression.

        In VMSS the upper layers were the freest: pre-intervention kept them safe, institutional presence guaranteed abundance, and cognitive privacy preserved the inner life. Here the upper layers thought cautiously, stagnated creatively and kept their social harmony through neurological self-censorship. Nobody with social standing watched -3's displays, and its terminal population had nothing left to lose from a visible thought, so -3 was the only layer where cognition ran in its natural, unconstrained state. The -3 traces looked like Rhea's own baseline from before the switch.

        The VMSS alliance's statement was unanimous: cognitive transparency was incompatible with treaty membership. The alliance framework required structural compatibility between gradient governance models, and in VMSS cognitive privacy was a charter-level right. The statement quoted Article II, cognition is non-public and carries no penalties or gains, and the whitepaper's rights boundaries: a civilization that monitors conduct must be explicit about what it does not monitor, or it becomes the surveillance state it claims to replace. Jonah read it aloud at breakfast, and Rhea saw his opinion of it on his display before he reached the end.

        Several alliance nations that had kept bilateral trade and diplomatic relations with Rhea's nation suspended them. Its ambassadors carried every strategic thought, reservation price and private assessment on their AR displays, and the alliance nations refused to send diplomats into an environment where a counterpart could read their internal deliberation in real time. Nobody sanctioned or embargoed her nation. It was excluded from every interaction that required one party to think privately: diplomatic exchanges, trade negotiations, intelligence-sharing. Meanwhile several authoritarian states expressed interest in the technology. They had spent centuries trying to learn what their citizens thought through crude surveillance.

        Over five years about 11% of the nation's Sanctuary-equivalent population and 6% of its Main Layer population emigrated to allied nations, primarily to VMSS. The papers compared this structural emigration with the Softer Ring's philosophical wave of 4,200. The emigrants were mostly creatives, researchers, entrepreneurs and anyone else whose professional output depended on unconstrained exploratory thinking. Their brains had stopped working properly under observation, and their cognitive function had degraded until they could not do their work, maintain their relationships or sustain their mental health in a transparent environment.

        In the fifteenth year Jonah said it at dinner.

        "I can't compose here."

        His display said it a moment before he did. They left that spring.

        At VMSS's border they went through the standard ledger audit and entered at the layer their behavioral records warranted. Most emigrants entered Main or Sanctuary; Rhea and Jonah entered Main. Within weeks the suppression began to lift. Spontaneous thought returned and creative capacity rebuilt. The chill had been an adaptive response to a surveillance environment, and without the surveillance it reversed. There was no permanent brain damage. The recovery was reported universally, and the emigrants in their building described it consistently, as thinking in color again after years of grayscale.

        Behind them the recalibration worked exactly as designed. The charter committee, which had built it as a radical extension of transparency and not as an instrument of control, watched it adopted by exactly the governments it least resembled, for exactly the purposes it least intended. Back there, deception was eliminated and crime was near zero. Every citizen knew exactly what every other citizen thought, the social contract was fully transparent, and no one could lie, manipulate or conceal harmful intent. The committee's argument was internally coherent, and the implementation was technically flawless. No authority was watching; everyone watched everyone. There was no one to reform or overthrow, and no policy to repeal. The architecture was democratic, voluntary and population-approved, and Rhea had voted for it.

        On a tram in Main a man across the aisle was reading. Rhea thought something unkind about his shoes, then turned an old problem from the lab upside down to see what it looked like wrong, then thought about her mother's garden, and she let each thought run as far as it went. He turned a page.

        Key lesson: VMSS's implant can read cognition, which makes Sanctuary's pre-intervention possible. The most important design decision in VMSS's architecture is that the implant shares none of it, so a person can have a thought and discard it unseen.

        Footnote — The Native Generation: The chill described here is transitional: it belongs to the first generation, people who had cognitive privacy and lost it. A second generation born into full transparency would never have known private thought, and for them a dark fantasy on a neighbor's AR display would carry the social weight of a cough. The desensitization would be complete. The upper layers would likely normalize through generational adaptation rather than remain cognitively suppressed indefinitely. If a residual cost persists, it shifts from cognition to intimacy: romantic relationships built under permanent mutual transparency develop a different architecture from relationships where productive ambiguity is possible. Whether that architecture is worse or simply different is a question the simulation's timeframe does not resolve.

        Marta Wessel's mother read the VMSS offer in 2026 and stayed on Earth. When Marta was nine she asked her why.

        "I disagreed with permanent reassignment," Ruth said.

        In VMSS's first decade there was only the one offer, and it came whole: permanent reassignment, no elections, the kill switch, cognitive privacy, merit-based governance and a 10:1 penalty asymmetry. Join or stay. Ruth's friend Carys had read all of it twice, accepted every trade-off and gone in the first year. Ruth agreed with eighty percent of the model and found twenty percent unacceptable, and that kept her home. A different twenty percent kept Marta's uncle home. "I didn't trust a system without elections," he told Marta. The woman downstairs said, "The kill switch made me uncomfortable."

        The government broadcasts of those years called VMSS a single ideological experiment: one architect, one model, one philosophy, untested and extreme. Parts of that were true, and that was why it worked. There was one model, and it was untested at civilizational scale. Permanence, no elections and remote lethality could each be defended alone. Together they gave any skeptic a reason to stay, and Ruth had found hers in the first.

        Ruth died at seventy-nine, a year under Earth's eighty-year lifespan ceiling, in a clinic with people sleeping in its doorway. By then Marta had spent most of her working life in a parole office under a wall chart that put the recidivism rate at sixty-five percent.

        Sixty years after the Founding Treaty, Marta printed a comparison sheet from an emigrants' forum. It had fourteen columns, one for each sovereign civilization in the alliance, spread over four continents. VMSS was the largest at 4.3 billion. The founding ally ran four rings for 1.8 billion. The other twelve ranged from 200 million to 1.6 billion, on four, five or six rings. The total at the bottom was about 14 billion, roughly 40% of Earth's total human population.

        The top rows read the same in every column: layered environments, behavioral consequence, AI governance, implant infrastructure, backup vessels, UBI, a charter-based constitutional framework. Below them no two columns matched. Marta read across looking for permanence. Her eye went past a nation that elected its leadership and one that had removed the kill switch and the nanobot plumes from its military doctrine. It went past six rings, with an achievement layer and a contribution layer as two distinct upper tiers above Main, and past backup vessels carried into a terminal layer with full revival. It stopped at the Recovery Gradient, which had replaced permanent reassignment with a measured recovery pathway, demanding but real. The last line bound all fourteen by treaty to mutual defense and free citizen movement.

        Her brother Anselm read the sheet upside down across his kitchen table.

        "It's one man's ideology," he said. The government had been saying it since Marta was a child.

        Marta turned the sheet toward him. It held fourteen charters from fourteen independent founding populations, and above the enforcement layer all fourteen governance structures differed.

        "Permanent reassignment is inhumane."

        She put a finger on the Recovery Gradient.

        "No democratic accountability, then."

        She moved it to the Elected Ring, where elected parliaments sat above the AI enforcement layer.

        "Remote lethality is authoritarian."

        The Softer Ring had constitutionally removed the kill switch. Anselm left the sheet where it lay.

        In the next room his daughter Pia had the evening feed on. She had grown up watching the alliance's civilizations, fourteen of them now, outperform Earth on lifespan, safety, medical access and material abundance at once. The emigration figures ran along the bottom of the screen: about 18 million people a year had left Earth for gradient nations in the decade when VMSS and two early allies were the only choices, and about 31 million a year in the decade since the alliance reached fourteen members with meaningfully diverse models. Almost all of the increase, the anchor said, came from holdouts with one specific objection, people who wanted gradient governance calibrated to the harshness they could accept. Then the feed ran a quiz, Which gradient nation matches your values? It had fourteen possible results and no option to stay. Pia took it twice.

        Marta had VMSS's consent form open on her own screen that night. It still said permanent layer reassignment with no appeal. She closed it and applied to the Recovery Gradient.

        She lived there ten years. She took the implant and drew the UBI floor, and her STI ledger filled with a decade of her conduct. She learned the rings and what sent people down them. The evening reports there described a -1 split in two: residents on the recovery track, gaming their STI to reach review, and residents who had given up on it. In her fourth year a notice reached Lio, across the hall, with the first word she'd had in years of the man who had defrauded her. He was eligible for recovery review, and she could give input. Lio looked him up. His STI ledger had nothing on it but good marks.

        "He was spotless while he was stealing from me," she said, and put the notice in a drawer.

        The Recovery Gradient's published results showed the deterrent eroding, with a breach rate measurably higher than VMSS's. Hanne, who had come over on the same crossing, read them and stayed.

        "It's the right balance for me," she said.

        In Marta's tenth winter the alliance published its internal migration tables, and they pointed the same way they had every decade. About 8% of the Earth emigrants who had entered through the softer nations moved on to VMSS within their first fifteen years. The Elected Ring was losing about 3% of its upper-layer population a decade to other alliance nations, disproportionately to VMSS and the founding ally. The Recovery Gradient lost about 2% a decade from its Sanctuary equivalent, the Softer Ring about 1.5%.

        Marta applied to VMSS that spring, for sharper consequence and higher precision, with no discretionary padding. In ten years VMSS had never sent her a word, and every comparison she had read came from somewhere else. At its border she brought a decade of alliance-standard behavioral data, and the ledger audit went through all of it. The officer scrolled without looking up.

        "Alliance transfers run better trajectories than people straight from Earth," he said. "Steadier, too."

        The audit placed her in Main, the layer her conduct warranted. He slid the consent form across. It was the form she had closed on Earth, word for word, and the thresholds behind it were the ones Ruth had read.

        "You understand the reassignment clause," he said.

        "Permanent, with no appeal," Marta said, and signed.

        By 2226, two centuries after the Founding Treaty, Marta still lived in Main, and a message reached her from Earth. Sefa, one of Anselm's descendants, was building the family record. No Earth lifespan ran the two centuries between Ruth's choice and Sefa's question, and Ruth had not lived to be asked.

        Ruth's answer is in the family file: she disagreed with permanent reassignment. But there were fourteen of them. One had elections. One had non-permanent reassignment. One removed the kill switch. One had six rings with finer gradation. One had full revival in every layer. She disagreed with all fourteen? Whatever she objected to, one of them had answered it at civilizational scale and published the results. I know what was out there. I'm asking anyway. Why didn't she go?

        Marta still had the comparison sheet in a drawer, soft at the folds. She took it out and smoothed it flat on the table before she began to write.

        Key lesson: A family of allied variants draws emigrants whom a single strict model does not reach. Some of them move on to VMSS after living under a softer one.

        Lenka Halvard took a Main Layer flat on Harrow Street in the Open Ring's first year. Through AR, every person on the street wore their implant's layer designation over their head, and the AI governance system applied that layer's institutional framework to them wherever they lived.

        The Open Ring was a five-ring ally founded on a large island continent. Its founders held that physical separation was a 22nd-century engineering necessity and no governance principle, so the nation adopted every element of VMSS doctrine except the mega-walls: implants, STI, AI governance, backup vessels, currency siloing, permanent reassignment, the kill switch, the UBI cascade. To the founders, walls were redundant infrastructure, spectacular, expensive and unnecessary once the implant could designate legal zones without them.

        In the first years people spread out mostly by self-selection. Lenka's tram ran up through Calloway, where the Sanctuary-equivalent families had gathered for its high trust density and the ambient safety of neighbors whose STI indicators glowed deep green. Main Layer households had settled all along the line. The lower-layer residents lived near its far end, around the markets where their currency circulated and their institutional framework operated. Most people chose to live near those who shared their currency, services and social norms. Harrow Street lay where the Main blocks ran into the -1 blocks.

        Lenka raised her son Otto there, next door to Dace Morrow. Her household had full medical coverage, full enforcement presence and full UBI, and so did Dace's until the sixth year, when he talked her into a housing cooperative that did not exist. He was reassigned to -1 for fraud and chose to stay in his flat. From then on he drew half the UBI, had partial institutional presence and lived under a different enforcement posture. His institutional services changed and his currency converted. The next morning Lenka saw his new designation, -1, over his head when he came out for his post. They still shared a sidewalk, a grocery store and a school catchment area.

        She called her sister that night. Ilka lived in VMSS. Years before, a man had assaulted her in Main, and he had gone to -2, behind a wall 15 kilometers high. Ilka had rebuilt her life in Main and never seen him again.

        "He's still next door?" Ilka said.

        "He's allowed to stay."

        Before the nation's first decade was out, the AI governance system began imposing geographic exclusion zones, ordering a reassigned citizen to relocate a minimum distance from their victim. Dace moved across the river. An enforcement officer came to show Lenka the zone on her map, a pale ring around Harrow Street. Dace's new flat was thirty minutes' walk outside it.

        "We monitor the ring," the officer said. "If he comes inside it, he's reassigned further."

        "And when do I find out?"

        "When we respond."

        "So he'd already be here."

        The officer didn't answer that. After he left, Lenka checked the map most nights.

        The spring after, Mila in Otto's class used her standing right to relocate out of -2. Her implant designation changed to Main, and her institutional framework with it. Her father, still designated -2, lived at the bottom of Harrow Street, inside the same school catchment area.

        "Here she'd have gone behind a wall," Ilka said on the next call. "He couldn't have followed her."

        By the twentieth year some of Harrow Street had moved away as neighbors sorted themselves again. Tobin, who ordered ahead of Lenka at the corner coffee shop most mornings, got his reassignment notification on a Tuesday. His UBI adjusted and his AR overlay updated. On Wednesday he was back in the queue, from the same flat, down the same street.

        "You all right?" Lenka asked.

        "It's a status change," Tobin said, and ordered.

        On the tram home Lenka read that year's national survey. Over two decades, the perceived severity of reassignment had declined measurably.

        The next summer Lenka visited Ilka in VMSS, and Ilka took her down the transit shaft into -1 for an afternoon. The sky was a different color there, under weather made by the wall's own microclimate. The buildings had a different character and the streets a different density of people, and the talk around them had a sound Lenka didn't know. The kiosk at the foot of the shaft wouldn't take Ilka's money. Behind them the wall rose fifteen kilometers of composite from a base a kilometer thick, tapering to a sheer crest.

        When she got home to Harrow Street, she opened the map and checked the pale ring before bed.

        Key lesson: Reassignment deters by moving a person into different surroundings. A change of legal status alone stops deterring, even with every institution working.

        In the Common Coin nation's second year, Teodor Vance, a Main Layer furniture maker, bought a load of timber in -2 for a fraction of its Main price and paid with the same money he spent at home.

        The nation was a five-ring ally that had adopted the full VMSS architecture with one economic modification. Its founders' argument was egalitarian: currency siloing created economic castes. Under siloing, a -1 resident's savings couldn't be used in Main, and a Main visitor to -1 arrived economically neutral and had to earn local currency from scratch. The founders saw that as punishment beyond the layer assignment itself, an economic wall stacked on the physical one, so their charter set a single currency, earned, spent, saved and transferred across all five layers without conversion. UBI was still graduated and taxation still layered, and asset liquidation still applied on punitive descent.

        That winter Teodor wrote his plan on one page. Main had full institutional presence: regulated markets, high taxation, comprehensive public services, and labor priced by people with a $10,000-a-month UBI floor. -1 had partial institutional presence, lighter regulation, lower taxes and a $5,000 floor. -2 had minimal institutional oversight, a $2,500 floor, and people whose economic behavior the harshness of their environment had shaped. The same currency bought at three price levels. Labor cost less in -2 than in Main, because the floor was lower and people were more desperate, and materials made in -2 cost less because the regulatory burden was lighter. A chair made in -2 and sold in Main would carry both savings.

        He rented a workshop in -2, sent money down the shafts for a crew at -2 wages and -2 timber, and sold the chairs that came up in Main at Main prices. His margin was the gap between people earning $2,500 a month and people earning $10,000.

        His adviser, Benedek, had grown up in VMSS. "You couldn't do this there," he said over the first quarter's figures. "Main money is no good in -2."

        By the tenth year the Main feeds were running profiles of residents who had built fortunes on lower-layer labor arbitrage without breaking a law, and one of them was Teodor.

        Benedek spent those years spreading Teodor's money out: a trust, the business signed over to his daughter Juno, stakes in cross-layer ventures, all in the one currency.

        "If anything ever happens," Benedek said, "they can only take what they can identify."

        In the twelfth year Teodor drove home drunk and was reassigned to -1. The standard liquidation took his accounts, his house and everything else in his name. It could not fully unwind what Benedek had spread across entities and relationships. Within a month Juno was sending money down every week, and the trust's income was reaching him through indirect channels.

        Benedek called at the end of the month.

        "In VMSS they'd have taken a hundred percent," he said. "You'd have landed with nothing, in a currency you'd never held, and learned the economy from zero. Nothing from Main would have converted."

        Teodor took the top floor of a building on Fenn Street in -1. Across the road Aurek, reassigned the same year for fraud, lived on the $5,000 UBI in two rooms over a laundry. He had come down with nothing behind him. More residents like Teodor arrived every year, and -1 had an economic elite of its own. Aurek carried his shopping home from the market. Teodor's was delivered.

        By the fifteenth year the charter committee had named what it was seeing: pre-VMSS Earth colonial economics. Its report described a wealthy layer that took labor and materials from a poor one, sold finished goods back to it at premium prices and kept the difference as profit. The founders, it noted, had set out only to remove what they saw as unnecessary economic segregation, and siloing existed to stop layer stratification from turning into economic colonialism. Teodor read it on Fenn Street and sent it to Benedek without comment.

        By the twentieth year the lower layers ran as extraction economies. In -2, the labor force, raw materials and lighter regulation had drawn sustained capital investment from Main entities, all of it legal across the shared currency. Rafe's crew in Teodor's old workshop, now in Juno's name, earned -2 wages in a Main-owned enterprise. No one coerced them, and the workshop ran efficiently. The profits went up the shafts in the same currency and stayed in Main, and the crew stayed in -2. That spring Rafe bought one of the crew's chairs for his mother, at the Main price.

        The committee took up reimposing siloing that year. The businesses it would disrupt came to about 12% of Main Layer's GDP, Juno's workshop among them. Juno called the hearings a witch hunt. The political will for reimposition did not materialize.

        Teodor watched the last hearing from his top floor on Fenn Street, with Juno's transfer for the week already in his account.

        Key lesson: Separate currencies keep a richer layer from profiting on a poorer layer's cheaper labor. They also keep wealth from softening a punitive reassignment.

        Solvei Brask was born in the first year of the policy and went from the delivery room to the Brenn Hill facility, where she lived until she was eighteen. Every child in the nation was raised in a centralized autoparenting facility like it from birth, whatever layer their parents occupied. Her file named a mother in -2. The Sanctuary children slept in the next dormitory, and all of them were fed, housed, educated, medically covered and behaviorally monitored identically.

        Their nation was VMSS-adjacent and ran a five-ring gradient model, and the founders' argument, cut into the atrium wall, was the logical extension of VMSS's own child protection doctrine. If children born in lower layers deserve the right to relocate to Main, the wall read, why leave the burden of exercising that right on the child? Why should a seven-year-old in -2 need to choose to leave their parent? Give every child the same start and let their own conduct sort them. The terms ran underneath in smaller letters. Parents in all layers kept visitation rights, custody did not exist, and at eighteen each citizen would be placed in the layer their own behavioral profile warranted.

        The facilities were well-designed and well-funded. The caregivers were AI-assisted and worked to strict developmental protocols, and the children's nutrition, education, socialization, medical care and emotional support were calibrated by developmental psychology rather than by the variable quality of individual parents. Solvei learned cooperation and the behavioral norms early. At bedtime a caregiver read her dormitory one story, as the protocol set. When she ran a fever at six, the night caregiver took her temperature on the hour, logged it, and moved on to the next bed.

        The founders' hypothesis was that removing parental variability from child development would produce more consistent outcomes, and every year the cohort's published figures confirmed it. On every measurable institutional metric, from literacy and health to behavioral stability and social development, the autoparented generation outperformed both Earth children and VMSS children raised by lower-layer parents.

        At eighteen the whole cohort's placements came through on one morning. Ninety-four percent went to Main Layer, Solvei among them. Three percent qualified for Sanctuary on early behavioral trajectory, and Marit, who had slept in the next bed for twelve years, was one of them. Three percent went to -1, almost entirely for conduct during their institutional upbringing itself; Rian from the east wing went for the fights. Nobody went to -2 or -3. It was the most uniformly well-behaved generation in the civilization's history, and the founders cited it as definitive validation.

        In Main Layer she met families. Dain, from the next bench at the survey office, had been raised by his parents. The first time he took her home, his mother Edda argued with him about his job through two courses, told the story of the winter he was four and cut his own hair, and brought up something from when he was twelve that sent him out of the room. He came back for dessert.

        "What were you like at four?" Edda asked Solvei.

        Solvei thought about it. "I was in the top decile for language."

        Dain laughed, and then saw that she meant it.

        Later, at the sink, Edda handed her a plate to dry. "He was my favorite from the day he was born," she said. "Don't tell his sister."

        They lasted two years. In the second spring the founders published the cohort's first adult figures: her generation formed romantic partnerships at a lower rate than the parent-raised and reported difficulty with attachment. The report found them psychologically sound, with no template for what unconditional attachment feels like. That summer Dain said he couldn't tell whether she would notice if he left, and she had no answer for him. He moved out in the autumn.

        Solvei took a caregiver's post at Brenn Hill at twenty-six. Between twenty-five and forty her cohort reproduced at about 60% of the rate of the parent-raised population. The shortfall was behavioral, since people raised without the experience of family were less likely to create families, and the nation placed no limit on reproduction.

        Marit's daughter was born in the thirty-first year of the policy and came to Brenn Hill the same day. Marit had a perfect behavioral record, a stable partnership and every institutional qualification for parenthood, and she carried the baby to the intake desk herself.

        "Corin's mother still calls him every morning," she said. "To ask what he's eating."

        Solvei took the baby and checked the band on her wrist.

        "I want to raise her," Marit said. "I don't know how. I'd learn."

        "You can visit her."

        "I know I can visit her."

        Five years later a caregiver from VMSS came to Brenn Hill on a visit: Kasimir Lund, from one of the autoparenting facilities in VMSS's Main Layer. The packet he left had VMSS's clause on its first page: children born in lower layers remain with their parents, and the right to relocate to Main Layer is a standing human right, exercisable at any age. The page called the weight this put on a child a real cost and accepted it as the price of keeping the bond between parent and child. Solvei read it at the intake desk while he waited.

        "So yours come to you," Solvei said.

        "When they decide to." He had grown up in -2 with his father. "I was seven when I understood I could go. I thought about it most nights for a year."

        "Why didn't you?"

        He took a while. "I liked him."

        Each year fewer of the caregivers on Solvei's floor had been raised by parents, and more had come out of the facilities, as she had. The older ones still did the voices when they read; Solvei read the words as written. The children on her floor were healthy and well taught, and Brenn Hill ran as well as it ever had.

        That night Marit's daughter asked for another story. Solvei checked the protocol and said goodnight.

        Key lesson: In VMSS the choice to move to Main belongs to the child, so that no parent in any layer loses a child by rule. Raising every child in an institution improves health, schooling and conduct, and weakens the family.

        Footnote — The Self-Correcting Policy: The 60% reproductive rate sets the policy's own expiration date. A nation that reproduces at 60% of replacement rate faces population contraction within two generations. The autoparented cohort's reluctance to form families is a demographic trajectory, and it forces the nation either to modify the policy or to import population from allied nations whose citizens were raised by parents. The Blank Slate repeals itself through demographics. The question is whether the charter committee recognizes this before the contraction becomes structural, or whether the policy's institutional momentum outlasts the population it was designed to serve.

        Dara Kolvey drew $10,000 a month, the same UBI as every citizen in every ring of her nation. It was a five-ring ally of VMSS, and its founders had equalized UBI across all layers on the principle that dignity should not be graduated. VMSS's halving cascade, $10,000 to $5,000 to $2,500 to $1,250, treated the reduction as part of the consequence. The founders held that the consequence was the layer: the environment, the enforcement posture, the institutional withdrawal, the social texture. A -2 resident already lived among people reassigned for predatory violence, with partial institutional presence and elevated revival failure rates, and halving their UBI on top of that would change their purchasing power without changing their behavior. The founders called that redundant punishment. From the first year it did what they intended: no resident of any layer experienced economic deprivation as part of their consequence.

        Dara kept the accounts at a freight broker in Main. In the nation's twelfth year her colleague Wenna was reassigned to -1, and her first message came back within the week.

        Same money, Wenna wrote. Same shops, same food. The clinic's farther and everyone on my street did something.

        The next spring Dara began moving small sums out of client accounts. She was caught in the fourth month and reassigned to -1.

        Her UBI stayed $10,000, paid in -1 currency. Currency siloing held, so money did not convert across layers, but within each layer the economic floor was identical. What changed for her was social and institutional: her institutional services were reduced, the enforcement posture on her street was different, and her neighbors' behavioral histories were the reasons they were there. She ate the same food, bought the same goods and used the same consumer economy as in Main. She ate well and lived comfortably, and when she took mornings at a bakery it was for something to do.

        Mateo, who had grown up in VMSS, came down to see her in her second month. He walked her street twice.

        "Where is everybody?"

        "Home, mostly. They don't have to work."

        "In VMSS's -1 people are running things," he said. "Stalls, repair shops, delivery crews. They're on five thousand, half of Main, and it's its own place."

        "Do they starve?"

        "Nobody starves in any ring." He looked at her door, the bakery bag, her new shoes. "There you'd have gone down four ways. Different ring, different people, different services, half the money."

        "This is Main with a different AR color scheme," Dara said.

        Her brother Joren was in -2, reassigned for violence years before, and in her third year in -1 she went down to see him. The economic constraint on VMSS's -2 was severe and deliberate, so -2 felt harsh in a way that reinforced the severity of the conduct that put people there. Joren got $10,000. His building was full of people reassigned for predatory violence, and a man followed her up two flights of stairs before he stopped. Joren's flat had a new kitchen and three locks on the door. He locked all three behind her without looking and went back to the stove.

        "In VMSS I'd be on twenty-five hundred," he said. "A quarter of Main. What's -1 like?"

        "Like moving to a worse neighborhood."

        In the twentieth year the founders published two decades of behavioral data. -1 reassignment rates ran about 6% higher than VMSS's: the marginal population that the full four-dimensional consequence would have deterred was not deterred by the three-dimensional version. The increase was modest, and the founders called it acceptable variance. The rate was not stable, though. It rose about 0.3% a year, as the population came to see -1 as Main Layer with less institutional presence but equal money. Dara read it on her front step. Wenna's message was still on her handset.

        Key lesson: UBI in every layer already guarantees dignity; VMSS lowers it at each ring so that a reassignment is felt in money as well as in surroundings. Without that, the step down to -1 deters fewer people each year.

        Footnote — The Tax Fork: The simulation leaves out a second-order problem that equal UBI creates for the tax gradient. VMSS's lower layers pay reduced tax rates because they receive fewer institutional services, partial taxation for partial institutions, and -3 Terminal sits at the bottom of the exact cascade (6.25% top marginal) as a civilizational membership acknowledgment rather than a redistribution mechanism. Equalize UBI and keep those rates, and the result is a financial inversion: a -2 resident receiving $10,000/month at a fraction of Main Layer's progressive tax rate has higher disposable income than a Main Layer resident receiving the same $10,000/month under heavy taxation. The punitive layers become economically preferable, and reassignment becomes a financial upgrade. Equalize the rates instead, and the injustice runs the opposite way: lower-layer citizens are taxed at Sanctuary and Main rates for institutional services they are not receiving: full taxation for partial institutions, which makes taxation punitive in the way the UBI equalization was designed to prevent. The founders face a fork with no clean exit. The UBI cascade was calibrated against the tax gradient, the institutional withdrawal schedule and the service-to-taxation ratio that makes each layer's economics internally coherent. Equalizing that one variable without the system it interlocks with produces a new category of unfairness that the original architecture did not contain.

        Hester Quill ran a fabrication shop in -2 of the Equal Floor nation, where every citizen in every ring drew $10,000 a month. The nation had kept VMSS's graduated institutional withdrawal: full services in Sanctuary and Main, partial in -1, reduced in -2, and the federal floor only in -3. It had kept the graduated tax structure as well, heavy and progressive in the upper layers to fund the full institutional apparatus, and reduced in the lower layers to reflect their reduced services. It had changed the income. Every layer now drew Main-Layer money, and the infrastructure had not changed with it. The economy of -2 had been built for $2,500.

        The tax question reached the founders in the first fiscal year, and it had no clean answer. VMSS reduced lower-layer tax rates because it reduced lower-layer institutional services, partial taxation for partial institutions, down to -3 Terminal at the bottom of the exact cascade: a 6.25% top marginal rate, kept as a civilizational membership acknowledgment rather than a redistribution mechanism. The founders had equalized income without equalizing institutional presence, and the tax gradient now sat on a contradiction. Hester watched the hearings on the screen above her bench.

        Option one kept the reduced lower-layer rates, and at -2's rate Hester would keep significantly more of her $10,000 than a Main Layer resident kept under heavy progressive taxation.

        "Put -1, -2 and -3 on Main-Layer progressive rates," one founder said. "Every citizen, whatever ring they live in. The financial inversion goes away, and disposable income comes out equal."

        That was option two, and under it Hester would pay Main's rate for a fraction of the infrastructure that Main's taxes funded. Another founder stood and read from VMSS's FAQ.

        "It would not be equitable to tax citizens at rates corresponding to services they are not receiving."

        "Keep the old rates," the first founder answered, "and a -3 resident under Terminal's minimal effective taxation keeps nearly all of their ten thousand."

        The founders chose option one. Taxation without corresponding services had historically produced civic resentment faster than almost any other policy failure. They reasoned that taxing citizens for services they did not receive was a more visible injustice than a tax-rate differential most citizens would never calculate. The calculation took about one news cycle. A Main commentator ran it on air the week the rates were published: the upper layers paid heavy taxes for the whole institutional apparatus, while the lower layers drew the same income with a fraction of the tax burden and none of the institutional overhead. The consequence architecture had inverted. The punitive layers were now the most economically advantageous layers in the civilization, and reassignment was a financial upgrade.

        "The founders set out to equalize dignity," the commentator said. "They've equalized income and built a tax shelter in the punitive rings."

        Hester's cousin in Main sent her the clip with one line: Room down there?

        Main Layer ran a fully serviced post-scarcity economy, built to absorb $10,000 a citizen at full institutional density. Hester's sister Fenna lived in -1, where about 60% of Main's institutional infrastructure was present and the economy had been designed for people spending $5,000 a month. Demand doubled overnight and supply did not change, so Main-Layer money was chasing -1-Layer goods. For two years Fenna's messages were cheerful. The shops were absorbing the extra spending and expanding their stock, and the one below her flat had hired two more people. In the third year the constraint turned structural. The automated medical clinics, fabrication-grade consumer goods and high-density entertainment infrastructure that Main had were not in -1 at all, because VMSS's graduated withdrawal had removed them.

        I've got ten thousand a month, Fenna wrote, and I can only spend six before the shops run out of things to sell me.

        In -2 the gap was severe. Institutional presence there was so reduced that private enterprise filled most civic functions, and a partially withdrawn hybrid economy calibrated for $2,500 now took in $10,000. In the service categories that needed institutional infrastructure to deliver, medical services, fabrication access, entertainment systems and automated retail, demand ran four times supply. Everyone on Hester's street could afford all of it, and the layer could not provide it. Her waiting list ran into the next season.

        In -3 the gap was total. Emrys, who had learned the trade at Hester's bench, maintained power and water lines there. VMSS's design had withdrawn daily governance from -3 and left the federal floor in place, and the economy was a capitalist frontier, with minimal institutional infrastructure scaled for $1,250 and no automated service delivery beyond basic utilities.

        Ten thousand a month, Emrys wrote, and almost nowhere to put it.

        The gap made the skilled artisan into essential infrastructure, at a scale VMSS's traditional architecture never produces. When Hester's cousin in Main wanted medical attention, he visited an automated clinic. In -2, anyone who wanted more than the reduced institutional baseline needed a private practitioner like Dr. Saye two streets over, one of a small number of qualified providers serving a population with $10,000 a month and acute demand. People like Saye, the pipe fitters, the engineer who kept the district's grid up and Hester herself had become the de facto economy of the lower layers. They were the only supply for institutional-grade demand, and every resident had Main-Layer money to pay them with. In VMSS's -2 economy a fabrication specialist like Hester would have earned a comfortable, unremarkable living. By the fifth year she was the most economically powerful person in her district. She set her prices and chose her clients, and she decided who received her services and at what quality.

        That year the feed ran the comparison. Prices had risen accordingly: a consultation with a practitioner like Dr. Saye cost three to four times what one cost in VMSS's -2, because demand at $10,000 a month overwhelmed a supply scaled for $2,500, and fees had settled where the two met. The equal floor had produced radically unequal purchasing power. A Main Layer resident's $10,000 bought a Main-Layer life. After -2's inflated prices, a -2 resident's $10,000 bought purchasing power comparable to or worse than VMSS's $2,500 baseline in the same layer.

        Odo, from the ground floor, brought in a cracked knee brace.

        "Eleven weeks," Hester said. "Or this week at double."

        "I get ten thousand a month, same as you."

        "So does everyone on the list." She turned the brace over. "The grid engineer's paying double."

        Odo took the brace back.

        By the fifteenth year each lower layer had divided into three economic classes, a stratification the founders had not anticipated, and Hester's street held all three. Hester drew the same $10,000 UBI as everyone else, and her service income multiplied it several times over; the artisans were accumulating wealth at a rate no other part of the layer could match. Odo's sister cut hair, a marketable skill though not an essential one, and it earned enough to offset the inflated prices. Odo had no specialized skill. He spent his $10,000 on inflated goods and services, and it bought him a floor well below what $10,000 bought in Main.

        The stratification was sharpest in -3, where, with minimal institutional infrastructure, virtually every service was private. Its private security operators, its medical providers and the engineers who maintained power and water, Emrys among them, had become a de facto governing class that controlled access to the services that made -3 livable. Every -3 resident's UBI was enough to bid for those services. It did not make the services exist in sufficient quantity to serve every bidder. The frontier capitalism VMSS's -3 produced under a $1,250 baseline now ran under a $10,000 one: the same economic dynamics at a higher price point, with the same winners and losers and the same stratification the founders had believed equal income would prevent. Emrys's neighbors had high nominal incomes and a low effective quality of life.

        I set the water price for the district now, Emrys wrote. Security and the doctors set theirs. Between us we take most of everybody's ten thousand.

        That spring Odo came up with another brace, cracked in the same place. Hester wrote his name at the bottom of the list.

        Key lesson: VMSS matches each layer's UBI to its taxes, its institutions and what its economy can supply. Money beyond that goes to whoever controls the supply.

        Maija Venn had lived in -1 for six years, for fraud, when her nation dissolved -2. It was an allied five-ring nation, and its founders had decided that the middle punitive layer, their equivalent of VMSS's -2, was redundant resolution. Three punitive layers overcomplicated the consequence architecture, they argued; two were sufficient, one for offenses that were harmful but containable and one for offenses that were terminal. They called it simplification without loss of function. The new four-ring model kept +1 Sanctuary with TIP and pre-intervention enforcement, Main Layer as the general population ring, -1 as the single punitive layer and -3 as the terminal layer.

        Maija's -1 held people sent down for financial fraud, as she had been, for sustained manipulation, for harassment patterns, and for single violent acts that had come to a natural stop. Wim, across the landing, had broken a man's jaw with one punch.

        "I hit him once," he told her the week she moved in. "Then I just stood there."

        The worst thing anyone on her stairwell had done was harmful but not physically dangerous in any ongoing way, and Maija walked home late without thinking about it.

        Her brother Nils was in -2. Outside a bar he had knocked a man down and kept hitting him after he fell, and the man went into a coma. In -2 institutional presence was diminished but present, and the $2,500 UBI was harsh but livable. Below it was -3, where governance was withdrawn and the economy ran on frontier capitalism, for people whose victims had not survived.

        "They still bring you back down here," Nils told her once. "About one time in a thousand they can't."

        The redistribution notice ran to one page. -2 would split along the severity gradient, at a severity threshold the founders believed cleanly separated its two halves. Lower-grade offenses, moderate violence, sustained physical intimidation and assaults that caused serious injury but stopped short of life-threatening harm, would go up into -1. Higher-grade offenses, severe violence, repeated predatory physical harm and assaults that left victims comatose or permanently impaired, would go down into -3. The redistribution was mechanical and immediate. Former -2 residents woke up in -1 or in -3, depending on which side of the threshold their conduct fell, and Nils woke up in -3. Kest moved into the flat below Maija's.

        Kest had put a man in hospital with repeated blows and had not stopped when he could have. Maija's UBI, the services on her street and the enforcement posture were exactly what they had been, and her placement had been calibrated for a -1 without residents like him. He shouted through the floor most nights, and in the spring he put a man from the fourth floor down the stairs. Maija started crossing the street when she walked home late.

        "I stole money," she told Wim. "I never hit anybody."

        Nils called from -3 in the first month.

        "They halved the UBI," he said. "Twelve fifty."

        "What about revival?"

        "Severed. If I die down here, I stay dead."

        "You didn't kill anyone."

        "The man across the hall did."

        The founders' ten-year review found the four-ring nation working. It outperformed Earth on every macro metric, and its citizens lived long, safe, materially abundant lives. Consequence still followed conduct, and the gradient still descended. Incident rates within -1 were up about 9% over the decade, driven entirely by the residents absorbed from -2, whose conduct profile included physical violence that -1's original population had not shown.

        Maija read the rest of the review at her kitchen table. The founders had called three punitive layers unnecessary resolution, and they had intended only to simplify. The review found that they had imposed the civilizational death penalty on conduct the five-ring model explicitly classified as severe but survivable. The decade's data said the spectrum between harmful impulse and fatal violence was too wide for two punitive layers. The four-ring model had no layer for conduct that went further than impulse but stopped short of fatal, so every offense from the middle was understated by -1 or overstated by -3. Neither misclassification was catastrophic, the review said, and both were persistent. Below Maija, Kest still shouted through the floor most nights.

        Every Sunday Maija called Nils in -3, and every Sunday he answered.

        Key lesson: Five rings is the fewest at which every boundary between rings marks a real change in enforcement, institutional presence and severity. With four, offenses between impulse and killing have no layer of their own.

        Anja Teller was one of the founders, and the line describing +2 in their announcement was hers: the recognition that sustained excellence deserves distinction beyond the Sanctuary threshold. Her nation, an ally of VMSS, was launching a six-ring gradient model. Below Sanctuary it matched VMSS exactly: Main Layer, -1, -2 and -3, with the same enforcement, the same institutional withdrawal and the same UBI cascade. Above Sanctuary the founders added +2, a ring gated at 92 STI and reserved for the highest-trust citizens in the civilization. Anja put the treaty application together herself. It carried architectural drawings of a sixth mega-wall, a dedicated +2 infrastructure plan, and a population projection of approximately 50 million residents drawn from the upper end of Sanctuary's behavioral spectrum.

        The alliance approved the application. Gradient governance with six rings still qualified, and the layer equivalence mapping was straightforward: for border protocol purposes, +2 mapped to VMSS Sanctuary. The nation built the wall.

        The founders had understood that +2 could not offer less than Sanctuary, since a ring above the highest ring cannot withdraw services, so +2 launched with every feature Sanctuary offered. Anja crossed the new mega-wall with the first residents into a place that was, by every measurable metric, identical to the Sanctuary they had just left. The economics were full post-scarcity, and her first monthly UBI deposit in +2 was $10,000, in the same currency as +1 and Main. The services directory listed TIP, the pre-intervention enforcement that halted harmful acts before completion, then full augmentation and longevity access, best-tier revival fidelity and full institutional services.

        The founders had expected that for year one. The plan was always to set +2 apart over time, and the differentiation roadmap was Anja's to run: five years to add exclusive research facilities, priority access to experimental technology, a governance consultation role and enhanced neural diving infrastructure. In year one +2 was Sanctuary with a higher number, and the founders called it the foundation phase.

        She began with the exclusive research facilities. Sanctuary already had collaborative research facilities, and restricting research access by STI bracket went against the meritocratic principle that research capability is credentialed by competence rather than behavioral score. The first admissions list shut out Viggo Lemm, an 87-STI researcher with a century of materials science experience, and let in a 93-STI resident with no research background. Viggo called her from Sanctuary the week it was posted.

        "What's behind your wall that I haven't got down here?" he said.

        "Nothing yet."

        "Then who is it for? The man you took has never worked a bench in his life."

        The rest of the roadmap went the same way. Beside the experimental-technology item in her draft she wrote: Meritboard already governs deployment, by competence and institutional need. An STI bracket has no relationship to who can use it productively. Beside the governance consultation role she wrote: Sanctuary already takes part in petition-based regulatory mechanisms. A 92+ gate makes an elite legislative class, gated by behavioral score instead of demonstrated policy competence.

        The 92 gate selected people who had scored seven points higher on the same metric, and by year five she had found no service, no facility, no institutional feature and no governance role that logically belonged behind it rather than behind the 85 gate Sanctuary already provided. The roadmap was quietly shelved.

        +2 ran as Sanctuary with a higher STI floor and a smaller population. The wall had changed the residents' address, and they lived identically to +1's, with the same enforcement, economics, institutional access, services, augmentation and revival. The only measurable difference was population density: 50 million behind the +2 wall, 250 million in +1. That year's residents' survey reported satisfaction with the intimacy and exclusivity of +2.

        By then the phrase had taken hold across the alliance as diplomatic shorthand: layer in name only. At a trade fair a buyer from another allied nation asked Anja how many rings her country ran.

        "Six," she said.

        "Five plus what?"

        Anja read the VMSS analysts' review of the comparative data. On citizen satisfaction, behavioral stability, creative output, institutional trust, longevity outcomes, revival success or any other metric, +2 differed from Sanctuary by no more than statistical noise.

        She felt it most in her own Selective Ascension Domain, +2's Cognitive Clarity Domain. The SADs were metric-gated micro-communities with functional lifestyle distinctions, the one mechanism that actually gave meaningful differentiation above Sanctuary, and the wall that was supposed to improve on them left them drawing on +2's 50 million instead of Sanctuary's full 300 million. A Cognitive Clarity Domain that sustained 40,000 residents from a 300-million base sustained approximately 6,500 from +2's pool. The micro-communities that had made the domains vibrant, with familiar faces within weeks, intellectual density and small-town cultural life, thinned toward the minimum viable population. Several SADs in +2 failed to reach critical mass and sat functionally dormant. In Anja's, the reading room kept its spare chairs stacked by the door.

        By year fifteen the consensus inside the nation's governance apparatus was that +2 did not justify its infrastructure, and Anja wrote the paper that said so. The mega-wall cost maintenance. The administrative overhead of a sixth ring (separate population tracking, separate infrastructure budgets, a border protocol that processed citizens crossing between two identical environments) produced no governance return. The rational decision was decommissioning: dissolve +2, reintegrate its population into Sanctuary, and acknowledge that five rings exhausts the meaningful structural gradients. On the paper's last page she wrote:

        A sixth ring above Sanctuary is architecturally incoherent. The five-ring model already delivers everything the architecture can provide at the Sanctuary threshold. TIP is the enforcement ceiling, post-scarcity the economic ceiling, full augmentation the health ceiling. A ring above Sanctuary would need to offer something that requires ring-level infrastructure: its own mega-wall, its own enforcement posture, its own economic baseline, its own institutional character. Nothing qualifies. SADs are the differentiation mechanism for any population filter above the Sanctuary baseline, and they filter on a functional criterion such as cognitive clarity, non-attachment or creative output.

        Ivar Stenmark, who had signed the founding document beside her, brought the paper back to her office himself.

        "Take the wall down and we tell everyone the founding architecture had a ring in it that served no function," he said. "That our signature divergence from the five-ring model was a wall between identical rooms, from the start."

        "It was."

        "Then that's what we'd be telling them." He left the paper on her desk.

        The nation did not decommission +2. The political cost of that admission exceeded the maintenance cost of the wall. Twenty years after the founding, the founding document still described +2 in Anja's words.

        That spring she went down to Sanctuary to see Viggo. At the crossing an officer ran her through the border protocol, logged her out of +2 and into +1, and asked the purpose of her visit.

        "Lunch," Anja said.

        Viggo was at his bench when she arrived, running a sample through the same furnace model the facility behind the wall had bought.

        Key lesson: Above Sanctuary's threshold, enforcement, economics, infrastructure and institutional services are already at their maximum, so a higher trust score has nothing left to unlock. Any further distinction has to come from small communities that select their members for a way of living.

        Tereza Holm was sworn onto one of the first judicial panels of the Human Bench, a VMSS-adjacent nation that operated a five-ring gradient model. The nation had kept the full VMSS technology stack: implants, STI, backup vessels, mega-walls, currency siloing, the UBI cascade, the kill switch. The implants still recorded behavioral data, the STI ledger still tracked trust, and the implant's non-repudiable record of what happened was still the evidentiary foundation. The founders replaced one thing, AI governance for consequence delivery. In VMSS the evaluation was mechanical and consistent: the AI governance system evaluated the behavioral data against established thresholds and produced a categorical determination, the same one for the same fully evaluated case in every district. Here a panel of three human judges reviewed the implant record, heard the citizen's context and rendered a determination.

        At the swearing-in one of the founders read out a passage from VMSS's charter: …the same consequence classification regardless of which district it occurs in… No judge weighs the case. No judicial discretion modulates the outcome. The absence of a judge is not a gap — it is the design. A few of the new judges laughed.

        "The implant tells you what happened," the founder said, "and you catch the nuance a mechanical evaluation flattens."

        For the first years the panels' output was, by several measures, superior to VMSS's automated system. Human judges handled edge cases that automated thresholds evaluated crudely. In the first year Tereza's panel heard Matthias Coyle, who had discovered his partner's infidelity and, under extreme emotional duress, struck her once, causing minor injury. The act technically met the threshold for -1 reassignment. The panel weighed the provocation, the severity, the absence of any pattern and Coyle's full behavioral history, and determined that the act, while qualifying under strict threshold analysis, did not warrant reassignment. They imposed an STI consequence and monitored rehabilitation, and Coyle remained in Main Layer.

        Afterward Benno Kray hung up his robe beside hers. "In VMSS he'd have gone to the AI," he said. "Article XII, multi-factor evaluation."

        "Which weighs context, severity, pattern and cumulative history. What we just did."

        "So it might have kept him in Main too. Or it might not." He buttoned his coat. "Put him in front of three panels here and you might get three different outcomes."

        Over the next decade the panels produced a measurable pattern the founders had not anticipated, a sympathy gradient, and Tereza watched it form at her own table. One spring the panel heard Linnea Ostberg, a surveyor from a Sanctuary family, who arrived with a prepared statement and put her conduct inside a story the judges found compelling. She was articulate, remorseful and easy to sympathize with. Three days later it heard Hakon Dray, a dock loader with little schooling, whose implant record showed the same act.

        "You've got the recording," Hakon said. "Watch it."

        Ostberg received monitored rehabilitation, and Hakon was reassigned. Tereza voted with the majority both times.

        After fifteen years the national data showed a statistically significant correlation between presentation quality and outcome, controlling for offense severity, behavioral history and all other variables. The gradient correlated with education, social class and articulacy. Citizens from upper-layer backgrounds who were evaluated for qualifying breaches presented more effectively: they were accustomed to institutional interaction, understood narrative framing and arrived at the panel better prepared. Citizens from lower-layer backgrounds, citizens with limited education and citizens who expressed themselves through anger rather than contrition presented less effectively. Tereza found Ostberg and Hakon in the appendix, two rows apart.

        The variability compounded as the system scaled. By then the nation operated approximately 4,000 judicial panels processing consequence evaluations across five layers, each composed of three judges drawn from a qualified pool. Judge quality varied, and so did panel composition. Regional judicial cultures emerged: panels in Tereza's district developed a reputation for leniency, and panels in another district one for severity. A citizen's outcome depended on what they had done, and also on which district they lived in, which panel heard their case and which three judges sat on it that day. The implant record, the non-repudiable account of exactly what happened, was the same in every courtroom.

        By year twenty-five, identical offenses produced reassignment in one district and monitored rehabilitation in another, at a rate that made the system's outputs partially dependent on geography. VMSS's automated, implant-level architecture made that structurally impossible. The nation had reintroduced every variable the charter passage read at Tereza's swearing-in was written to eliminate, down to how a citizen presents to the person evaluating them. That year's national report carried a case from the severe district in its annex.

        A woman there had discovered her partner's infidelity and struck him once. The injury was minor, and her record showed no pattern. Her panel had reassigned her to -1. Tereza pulled Coyle's file from the archive and laid the two implant summaries side by side.

        Key lesson: Human judges occasionally handle a single case better than automated evaluation. Their answers also shift with the panel, the district and how well the citizen speaks, so the same act can cost two people different amounts, which VMSS treats as unequal by definition.

        In the alliance's sixtieth year Agathe Lind, one of the founders of the Open Ring, covered the long wall of her study with a grid. The treaty framework now encompassed over twenty sovereign civilizations operating gradient governance models, and the diversity had become extraordinary. She gave each major design choice in VMSS's architecture a row and each nation a mark. Every row showed the choice modified, removed or replaced by at least one allied or adjacent nation:

        Walls: kept / removed. Reassignment: permanent / recovery pathway. Leadership: merit-based governance / elected officials. Kill switch: kept / removed. Currency: siloed / single. Children: parental child-rearing / universal autoparenting. Judgment: AI governance / human judges. UBI: halving cascade / equal.

        No central authority had designed this diversity. It had emerged through twenty sovereign acts of founding, each led by a population with genuine philosophical commitments to its specific divergence. The Open Ring's founders, Agathe among them, genuinely believed walls were redundant. The Common Coin's founders genuinely believed currency siloing was punitive, and the Blank Slate's genuinely believed institutional childhood would outperform parental childhood. None of them had been cynical, and none had been foolish. They were civilizational designers who disagreed with specific VMSS design choices, adopted what they agreed with and modified what they didn't, and between them they had built an experiment nobody had set up with controls: a living laboratory of gradient governance variants at civilizational scale, running on real populations and producing real consequences and real data.

        The bottom half of the wall was the data. After decades of operation, the comparative data across the alliance showed a pattern that no single nation's experience could establish. Every divergence from VMSS's traditional architecture produced measurable costs in the specific dimension the divergence modified, while the unmodified dimensions continued to function as designed. The costs were not catastrophic. Every allied nation was a functional civilization that outperformed Earth on every macro metric: lifespan, safety, material abundance, institutional trust. The costs were consistent, directional and compounding. Under each nation's name she wrote what its divergence had cost:

        The Softer Ring removed the kill switch. A defense gap: eleven deaths over fourteen weeks when conventional security could not match the capability remote neutralization provides.
        The Elected Ring introduced democratic governance. Discretionary padding between event and consequence: a 14% higher breach rate over twenty years.
        The Recovery Gradient allowed upward reassignment. Deterrent erosion, victim re-traumatization, and STI gaming for recovery eligibility.
        The Drift Vessel achieved 100% revival. Progressive identity erosion in lower layers, where multiple revivals compound drift into de facto personality replacement.
        The Glass Mind made cognition transparent. Civilizational cognitive suppression, creativity collapse, and an inverted layer gradient in which the upper layers became the most mentally constrained.
        The Open Ring removed walls. Consequence became administrative rather than environmental, reducing deterrent weight.
        The Common Coin unified currency. Cross-layer economic extraction indistinguishable from colonial economics.
        The Blank Slate universalized autoparenting. The family as an institution, reproductive decline, and a generation that cannot love irrationally.
        The Equal Floor equalized UBI. The economic dimension of consequence, producing lower layers materially indistinguishable from Main; compounded by the Spending Ceiling effect, where equal income poured into degraded institutional infrastructure produced inflationary spirals, artisan monopolies, and purchasing power that converged back toward the original baseline despite quadrupled nominal income.
        The Human Bench replaced AI governance with judges. Inconsistent outcomes correlated with presentation quality, geography and panel composition.
        The Compressed Ring dissolved the middle punitive layer into a four-ring model. Upward contamination of -1 with moderate-violence offenders, a downward cliff-drop of severe-violence offenders into terminal -3 with severed revival, and the loss of the architectural vocabulary for conduct between impulse and fatality.
        The Layer in Name Only added a sixth ring above Sanctuary. Twenty years of maintaining a wall between identical rooms, kneecapped SAD populations, and a ring that could not be decommissioned because admitting it was empty was more expensive than keeping it staffed.

        Twenty nations had set out to improve VMSS's design choices. Each had independently identified features it believed were too harsh, too rigid, too automated, too secretive, too permanent or too punitive, and built a civilization that addressed those specific concerns. Every modification was principled, and every one was defensible. Every one produced the outcome its founders wanted and an additional outcome they did not want, with measurable downstream effects the traditional model did not exhibit. In the autumn Agathe added a right-hand column for the additional outcomes and headed it A reduction in the clarity, consistency or weight of consequence:

        Kill switch removal: reduced military deterrent. Recovery pathway: reduced reassignment permanence. Elected officials: reduced enforcement immediacy. Wall removal: reduced environmental separation. UBI equalization: reduced economic weight. Human judges: reduced categorical consistency.

        VMSS had not predicted these outcomes, and it had not designed the alliance to serve as a comparative experiment. But the alliance's diversity had produced something no internal simulation or pressure test could: civilizational-scale evidence that the traditional architecture's harshest features (permanence, walls, automated judgment, the kill switch, the UBI cascade, currency siloing, cognitive privacy, child autonomy) were load-bearing elements of an integrated system, each reinforcing the others, rather than independently harsh design choices that could be softened one at a time. Remove one and the system continued to function. The load redistributed. The remaining features bore more weight than they were designed for, and the system drifted, slowly, consistently and in the same direction every time: toward softer consequence, reduced deterrent, and measurable divergence from the model that modified nothing. Agathe had watched it happen at home.

        Dagny Sorel, who had helped found the Blank Slate, came to see the wall that autumn week and read down the new column with her finger.

        "Every one of them went softer," she said.

        "Where each of us meant it to," Agathe said.

        By spring the alliance's diversity was VMSS's greatest strategic asset and its most effective argument. It widened the emigration funnel, gave hesitant populations an on-ramp and collapsed Earth's counter-narrative, and at the same time it validated the specific model it had been created to improve upon. Twenty nations had tried to build a better version of the architecture, and between them they had shown that the original was better, by producing the specific costs its traditional features were designed to prevent. It was the most comprehensive validation of VMSS's design philosophy ever produced, and the nations that had tried to prove VMSS wrong had written it.

        That spring Dagny brought over a VMSS review of the alliance, with Agathe's own tables in its citations. The traditional model's harshest features were validated by the measured consequences in allied nations that had tried operating without them. Every allied nation that had softened a specific feature had produced a published, verifiable, decades-long dataset showing what that softening cost, and the review used them. The review made no case of its own. For permanence it cited the Recovery Gradient; for cognitive privacy, the Glass Mind; for walls, the Open Ring.

        "They cite your wall," Dagny said. Agathe pinned the review to the wall under the Open Ring's row.

        Key lesson: VMSS's harshest features support one another, so a nation that softens one shifts the weight onto the rest and drifts toward weaker consequence over decades. The clearest evidence for this came from the allies who tried it.

        Footnote — The Uncomfortable Corollary: If the alliance's diversity validates the traditional model through variant failure, then the variants are more valuable to VMSS as imperfect implementations than they would be as perfect copies. A world of twenty identical VMSS nations produces no comparative data. A world of twenty divergent nations produces the only evidence that could prove the traditional architecture correct, generated by civilizations that tried to improve it and measured the cost. The uncomfortable implication is that VMSS benefits from allied nations making choices it considers wrong, because without those choices the argument for the traditional model remains theoretical.

        Brenna Kast worked the safe shafts in a -3 mining district, and she was on shift the week Kofi Asante arrived. He was a Main Layer industrial engineer on a six-month visitation contract, with skills the terminal economy needed: fabrication maintenance, power cell diagnostics, structural assessment for mining operations. He arrived economically neutral, earned freedom tokens through legitimate work, and built a reputation in his district's repair cooperative within weeks. He fixed the cage lift on Brenna's shaft in an afternoon.

        Three months in, a tunnel collapse killed two native miners, and Kofi was buried with them. Brenna was on the crew that dug the three of them out. The two native miners were gone permanently, and she carried one of the coffins up to the ridge. Kofi revived in a Main Layer medical bay four hours later, filed a re-entry application and was back in -3 within the week: same district, same reputation, same employer, same knowledge of the tunnel system. He found her in the canteen at the end of a shift.

        "I heard about the funerals," he said. "I'm sorry."

        "You were under there with them."

        "Four hours," Kofi said. "Then I woke up in Main."

        Kofi, who meant nobody harm, was a competent engineer who had noticed that his backup vessel made him the most valuable miner in the district, and his employer had noticed too. Within a year Kofi was running the most dangerous shaft in the operation, the one that produced the highest-grade ore and killed a worker every few months. He earned four times what native miners earned on the safe shafts, Brenna among them, because he took the shifts nobody else survived. He died twice more and came back twice more. His employer's output doubled.

        Word traveled up to Main. One evening Kofi showed her the figure on a Main Layer feed: within eighteen months, over twelve thousand Main Layer citizens had filed -3 visitation applications. The gold rush had begun.

        The visitors fanned out across -3's economy. Mining, hazardous construction, deep-water salvage, chemical processing: every industry where mortality risk was a material cost discovered that visitors eliminated that cost entirely, and the economic distortion was immediate and structural. Dangerous-labor wages collapsed. Native miners who had once commanded premium pay for mortal risk found themselves competing against visitors who would do the same work for less, because the visitors carried no risk premium. Some operations fired their native crews entirely and replaced them with rotating visitor teams, and Brenna's was one of them. She tried a demolition crew, a salvage boat and a chemical works on the coast, and every hiring table quoted her the visitors' rate. Private security firms were recruiting visitors as enforcers who could not be permanently killed. On -3's wild side, whose entire economic logic depended on the finality of death, Colosseum-classified enterprises found that visitors would enter at any odds, because the odds did not apply to them; a visitor who died in the Saurian Park revived in Main Layer with a story and a re-entry application.

        Her brother Cato fought on the district circuit, where the Colosseum gate money rested on the genuine scarcity of people willing to risk permanent death, and the Colosseum economy had flooded with visitors for whom the risk was theatrical. Before he fought a visitor, Brenna sat with him in the back room while he wrapped his hands.

        "He told me at the weigh-in he's booked a table in Main on Friday," Cato said. "Win or lose."

        Brenna paid at the gate that night, and the price had dropped again. The fight circuits had become exhibitions rather than existential contests, and Cato fought for his life against a man who faced, at worst, a four-hour interruption. She watched from the rail.

        By then natives like Brenna could pick out visitors on sight. The confidence was different, and the risk calculus was visible in how they moved. Protests emerged in the mining districts. Someone painted DEATHLESS GO HOME along the ore sheds while Brenna held the ladder. Reputation-ledger associations began flagging visitor status, and some districts attempted to restrict visitor entry through cooperative access rules. Private security firms loyal to native employers threatened visitors physically, but every instrument -3's organic justice system had was calibrated for a population where death was final.

        Sella, who ran nights for one of those firms, told Brenna about the visitor they had locked in the storeroom under the weigh station.

        "Dead by morning," Sella said. "Did it himself. He's eating breakfast in Main." She drank. "Kill one and he's back inside a week."
