        The site was a pre-reassignment chemical works two ridges beyond the perimeter: condenser coil and tantalum fittings, a haul that would pay for a quarter if she reached it before the scrap crews did. She stayed too long in the cracking shed weighing fittings. When she came out into the late light, five people stood between her and her vehicle.

        None of the five had bothered to cover their faces. No drone was coming; the layer ran no monitoring. Her sidearm was on her hip. In -3 a death was final, hers or theirs, and she left it where it was.

        They did not kill her. Within the hour their leader, a flat-eyed woman the others called Sorrel, was checking Calla's name against the regional reputation ledgers. Calla watched her read. Partway down the second screen, Sorrel's shoulders came down.

        "Four guarantors," Sorrel said. "Eleven years of trade. Member in good standing." She turned the handset around so Calla could see her own column of numbers, the one that decided whether the market would sell to her. "You'll be fine."

        "You don't know that."

        "I know your association." Sorrel was already drafting the note. "They'll pay. They can't have the market hearing they didn't."

        The note went out that evening to the district association, payable in freedom tokens.

        The crew was eight people living in the shell of a pumping station. The two who cooked still argued about stall prices like traders; their voluntary districts had thrown them out. A heavyset man on the night watch had come down on a -3 ledger, and so had the two who played cards with him. Nobody asked them what for. The other three had no records anywhere, and never talked about it.

        A man was chained at the far end of the pump hall. He was sixty-some, with no implant, a punitive arrival from years back. He hauled water and fed the generator, and as near as Calla could tell he had been doing it for two years. He slept eight meters from her. That first night she asked him whether they had ever sent a note for him.

        "Who to?" he said.

        "I'm owed money, though," he said after a while. "Federal. Goes into an account every month."

        "Where do you draw it?"

        "Distribution point." He lifted the chain an inch off the concrete and let it drop. "Two ridges that way."

        She lay awake a long time. Her association's budget had a line headed Persistent threats. It paid bounties to the retrieval contractors, and for crews that graduated from robbery to killing district members it paid for places in the private supermax the eastern associations kept between them. She wondered whether Sorrel had ever seen it.

        They fed her, mostly. On the second night she tried the door, and a crewman hit her once across the face before Sorrel said something short to him. Nobody touched her again.

        On the ninth morning they walked her to a dry creek crossing. Two association security vehicles were parked on the far bank. A contracted negotiator came halfway across and read the token transfer aloud, figure by figure, until Sorrel nodded.

        "Tell your association we fed you," Sorrel said.

        Calla walked the last forty meters herself. Her legs held until she was inside the vehicle.

        Nobody was arrested; past the markers, nobody could be. The association logged the crew, raised the outskirts advisory rating for the eastern ridges, and added nine percent to her recovery cost as a member-services levy, paid off over four quarters. The market reopened to her that same week. Her vouchers held.

        She went back for the old man. It took her a season to talk the association into it.

        "There's no levy line for this," the officer in member services kept telling her. "Who's the member? Who's the counterparty?"

        In the end she funded the retrieval contract herself, out of the profit the tantalum job was supposed to bring.

        The contractors found the pumping station empty. The crew had moved on the week after the exchange and taken their labor with them. The contract came back with a report and a refund of the unused completion fee.

        She still works the salvage trade, and she doesn't cross the markers anymore. New arrivals at the market talk about the ridges the way she used to. When they ask whether she regrets coming down, she says no. Then she unfolds the map and shows them where the patrol contracts end.

        Key lesson: In the -3 outskirts, protection from other people is private and commercial. It reaches only those someone will pay to get back.

        Every part of the system did its job. That is the part Mara Ellison cannot get past.

        She was assaulted in a Main Layer transit underpass at the wrong hour by a man whose failsafe was disabled — which was his right, in the layer where the failsafe is yours to configure. The implant logged the act at neural resolution as it happened. It did not stop him, because in Main the default is post-intervention: harm may complete, and the civilization answers afterward with its full weight. It answered with its full weight. The medical drone reached her in under a minute, the nanite injectors closed the physical injuries before she had finished understanding what had happened, and the neural-therapy module began its work at the scene. Within the hour the three-axis evaluation had run — severity, pattern, the reversibility axis reading the harm as irreversible regardless of the body's restoration — and the man was through the wall to -2 before the bruising she no longer had would have surfaced. No trial. No testimony required of her. The architecture had better evidence than her memory from the first second. By every metric the civilization tracks, her case is a clean execution of doctrine. The victim restored, the perpetrator consequenced, the ledger closed.

        And the act still happened to her. That is the sentence she keeps arriving at. The system was not slow, not unjust, not under-resourced. It was correct. And correct still meant it let the thing complete before it answered, because answering-afterward is what Main is — the proving ground, the layer of lived trust, the place where agency is preserved including the agency to do harm. She had read all of it. She had believed all of it, in the abstract way a person believes the architecture of the world they were born safe inside. She did not believe it anymore. Not because it failed her. Because it worked, and working was not enough, and nobody had ever told her those could be different things.

        The thing that sharpens it to a point is the wall itself — fifteen kilometers of composite blade that she can see from her district, behind which sits Sanctuary, where her case is not a category that exists. In +1 the Threshold Inhibition Protocol runs mandatory and continuous: a raised hand stops mid-motion, the muscles simply cease to answer, the act does not complete. No citizen of Sanctuary becomes the victim of another. That is the second founding line, and it is not aspiration there, it is plumbing. The civilization already knows how to make her assault impossible. It chose, for Main, to know how and not to do it — to hold the protection on the far side of a threshold she had not yet earned, in a layer whose entire identity is that you are trusted with the freedom to be harmed. The capability exists. The wall is where it stops. She started attending the coalition meetings the month her therapy tapered.

        The petition was not hard to surface. That surprised the upper-layer commentators and surprised no survivor: the signatures came fast, because the argument is almost unbearably simple once someone says it out loud. TIP already exists in Main as an opt-in — anyone can switch on the coverage Sanctuary gets by default. Make it mandatory, the petition said, and non-disableable, for this one category, the one harm the architecture's own doctrine calls irreversible no matter how whole the body comes back. Mara stood up at the third district meeting and said the line that ended up in half the campaign material: "It costs nothing to anyone who would never do it. It costs everything to the person it's done to. I have done that arithmetic with my own body. Tell me which side of it you're standing on." The expert panel scoped the trigger carefully — overpowering force, incapacitation, refusal-then-continuation, nothing that could mistake intimacy for assault. The dual-key challenge that tried to bury it in the amendment gauntlet was denied; the petition was the layer's own to decide. It went to the ballot clean.

        It failed at seventy-nine percent. One point of the eighty it needed, held back not by anyone who thought what happened to her was acceptable — the same population had voted survivors the right to release the memory of it only months before — but by people who could not bring themselves to make the implant a thing that overrides its bearer with no switch to turn it off. Mara sat with the number for a long time. The dissent, read honestly, was not her enemy: it was the proving ground refusing to stop being the proving ground, the layer declining to convert itself into Sanctuary one category at a time, the autonomy that lets a man disable his failsafe being the same autonomy that protects everyone else's right to govern their own skull. She understood it. She even, on the worst-honest days, agreed with a sliver of it. She just could not make the sliver weigh as much as the underpass.

        The coalition is already redrafting. Not a mandate this time — an opt-out-able default: every Main resident enrolled in the coverage at birth, disabling it a deliberate, logged, friction-heavy choice instead of the silent opt-in nobody remembers they declined. It would catch almost everyone the mandate would have caught, and leave the switch in the bearer's hand, which is the whole of what the seventy-nine percent was protecting. Mara thinks it will pass in a few years. She thinks it should. She is also clear-eyed that it would not have stopped the man in the underpass, who disabled his failsafe on purpose, and that no version of this short of the mandate she lost would have. That gap — between the protection the population will accept and the protection that would have saved her — is the thing she has decided to spend the next decades of her long life standing inside, because someone has to, and she is already there.

        Key lesson: The architecture's hardest cases are not the ones where it fails — they are the ones where it works exactly as designed and the correct outcome is still unbearable. Main is post-intervention by founding design: harm may complete, agency preserved, consequence answered afterward. For irreversible harm in a thousand-year life, "answered afterward" and "prevented" are not the same mercy, and the civilization already runs the prevention one layer up. LP-062 was the population being asked whether it would convert that capability into a mandate — and declining, not because the harm is tolerable but because a non-disableable override is a power Main will not hand its own implants even against its gravest wound. The protection goal won the room and lost the vote. The gap between what a population will mandate and what would actually have saved the victim is where the next law gets written.

        Return path: review the layers, revisit system architecture, or begin the entry process.

        No simulations match that search. Clear the box or widen the filters.
